Figure 1.
After completing the construction, we have a tree of Rellich functions
\{\mathcal{C}_{n}\}_{n\geq 0}
. The superscript
(j),j\in\{1,2\}
, of each child indicates the resonance type relative to the parent.

1.5.
Organization of the paper

The paper is organized as follows. We will introduce some basic notation and foundational lemmas for use throughout the paper in §
2
. The center arguments of this paper, constructing the Rellich functions and establishing Green’s function estimates via a multi-scale induction analysis are presented in §
3
. In §
4
, we employ the results from §
3
to finish the proof of the Main Theorem.

2.
Preliminaries

In this section, we explain the meaning of some notation and collect the foundational lemmas in this paper. The proofs of these lemmas can be found in Appendix.

Remark 2.1
.

Without loss of generality, we assume that
\theta_{M}
is the maxima point and
\theta_{m}
the minima one of
v
. Since we are considering small
\varepsilon
, we further assume that there exists some
a
with
0<a<\|\theta_{M}-\theta_{m}\|/10
, such that
|v^{\prime\prime}(\theta)|>3
in
\{\theta\in\mathbb{T}:\ \|\theta-\theta_{M}\|<a\}\cup\{\theta\in\mathbb{T}:\ \|\theta-\theta_{m}\|<a\}
, and
|v^{\prime}(\theta)|>3
in
\{\theta\in\mathbb{T}:\ \|\theta-\theta_{M}\|\geq a\}\cap\{\theta\in\mathbb{T}:\ \|\theta-\theta_{m}\|\geq a\}
. Under these assumptions, we denote

D=\sup_{\theta\in\mathbb{T}}\max(|v(\theta)|,|v^{\prime}(\theta)|,|v^{\prime\prime}(\theta)|).

We denote by
\Lambda_{L}
the set
\{x\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}:\ \|x\|_{1}\leq L\}
for any
L>0
, and
A\lesssim B
if
A\leq C(d,\tau,\gamma,D)B
, where
C(d,\tau,\gamma,D)
is an absolutely constant only depending on the dimension
d
, Diophantine parameters
\tau,\gamma
and the
C^{2}
norm of the sampling function
v
.

We now list some important lemmas, whose proofs are appended.

We begin with a lemma for finite-dimensional self-adjoint operators, which is useful to show the existence of eigenvalue near a certain energy
E^{*}
.

Lemma 2.1

(Trial wave function)
.

Let
H
be a self-adjoint operator on a finite dimensional Hilbert space and
E^{*}\in\mathbb{R}
. If there exist
m
normalized orthonormal functions
\psi_{k}\ (1\leq k\leq m)
such that
\|(H-E^{*})\psi_{k}\|\leq\delta
for some
\delta>0
and all
1\leq k\leq m
, then
H
has
m
eigenvalues
E_{k}\ (1\leq k\leq m)
counted in multiplicity satisfying
\sum_{k=1}^{m}(E_{k}-E^{*})^{2}\leq m\delta^{2}
. We call these
\psi_{k}
trial functions.

As a corollary, Lemma
2.1
immediately gives us

Lemma 2.2
.

If there exists a trial function such that
\|\psi\|=1
and
\|(H-E^{*})\psi\|\leq\delta
, then
H
has at least one eigenvalue in
|E-E^{*}|\leq\delta.
If there exist two orthogonal trial functions such that
\|\psi_{1}\|=\|\psi_{2}\|=1,\langle\psi_{1},\psi_{2}\rangle=0
and
\|(H-E^{*})\psi_{1}\|\leq\delta,\|(H-E^{*})\psi_{2}\|\leq\delta
, then
H
has at least two eigenvalues in
|E-E^{*}|\leq\sqrt{2}\delta
.

Next we provide a lemma concerning
C^{2}
Morse functions. This lemma, not only together with Diophantine condition, provides crucial separation of the resonant sites in our induction but also gives an upper bound estimate of the measure of bad phases in proving localization.

Lemma 2.3

(Properties of Morse function)
.

Let
E(\theta)\in C^{2}([a,b])
with a critical point
\theta_{s}\in(a,b)
. Suppose that there exists
\delta>0
such that
|E^{\prime}(\theta)|\leq\delta
implies
|E^{\prime\prime}(\theta)|\geq 2
with a unique sign for these
\theta
,
that is, if we denote
X:=\{\theta\in[a,b]:\ |E^{\prime}(\theta)|\leq\delta\}
, then either
\inf_{\theta\in X}E^{\prime\prime}(\theta)\geq 2
or
\sup_{\theta\in X}E^{\prime\prime}(\theta)\leq-2
. If
\theta_{1}
and
\theta_{2}
are both on the right of
\theta_{s}
or both on the left of
\theta_{s}
, then

\left|E\left(\theta_{2}\right)-E\left(\theta_{1}\right)\right|\geq\frac{1}{2}\left|\theta_{2}-\theta_{1}\right|^{2}

provided
|\theta_{1}-\theta_{2}|\leq\delta
.
Moreover,

|E^{\prime}(\theta)|\geq\min(\delta,|\theta-\theta_{s}|).

The final lemma that we recall is a classical computation of derivatives of simple eigenvalue for
C^{2}
one-parameter families of symmetric matrices.

Lemma 2.4

(Feynman-Hellman formulas)
.

Let
H(\theta)
be a family of finite dimensional self-adjoint operators with
C^{2}
parametrization. Assuming
E(\theta^{*})
is a simple eigenvalue of
H(\theta^{*})
and
\psi(\theta^{*})
is its corresponding eigenfunction, then
E(\theta),\psi(\theta)
can be
C^{2}
parameterized in a neighbourhood of
\theta^{*}
. Moreover, for
\theta
belonging to the neighborhood, we have:

(1)
.

E^{\prime}=\langle\psi,H^{\prime}\psi\rangle
.

(2)
.

E^{\prime\prime}=\langle\psi,H^{\prime\prime}\psi\rangle-2\langle H^{\prime}\psi,G^{\perp}(E)H^{\prime}\psi\rangle
, where
G^{\perp}(E)
denotes the Green’s function
(H-E)^{-1}
on the orthogonal complement of
\psi
.

(3)
.

Let
\mathcal{E}\neq E
is another simple eigenvalue and
\Psi
its eigenfunction, then we have

\langle H^{\prime}\psi,G^{\perp}(E)H^{\prime}\psi\rangle=-\frac{\langle\Psi,H^{\prime}\psi\rangle^{2}}{E-\mathcal{E}}+\langle H^{\prime}\psi,G^{\perp\perp}(E)H^{\prime}\psi\rangle

where
G^{\perp\perp}(E)
denotes the Green’s function
(H-E)^{-1}
on the orthogonal complement of the space spanned by
\psi
and
\Psi
.

3.
Multi-scale analysis

In this section, we will inductively construct collections of Rellich functions
\{\mathcal{C}_{n}\}_{n\geq 0}
. Each element
E_{n}\in\mathcal{C}_{n}
is a locally defined Rellich function of the
n
-scale restriction operator
H_{B_{n}}
. The definition of
E_{n}(\theta)\in\sigma(H_{B_{n}}(\theta))
depends on a certain element
E_{n-1}\in\mathcal{C}_{n-1}
, which is called the parent of
E_{n}
. The main aims of this section are

(1).

Construct the collections of Rellich function
\{\mathcal{C}_{n}\}_{n\geq 0}
by a multi-scale induction argument.

(2).

Verify that the Rellich functions inherit the Morse condition and two-monotonicity interval structure from the sampling function
v
, thus we can employ the properties of Morse functions from Lemma
2.3
.

(3).

Describe the resonance (operator norm of Green’s function) by these Rellich functions and establish Green’s function estimates for nonresonant sets with regularity condition.

Let
\varepsilon_{0}>0
be sufficiently small and
0<\varepsilon\leq\varepsilon_{0}
. Define
\delta_{0}:=\varepsilon_{0}^{\frac{1}{20}}
.

3.1.
The initial scale

The
0^{\text{th}}
generation of Rellich functions is defined as
\mathcal{C}_{0}:=\{v\}
. We first establish Green’s function estimates on
0
-nonresonant sets by a Neumann series argument.

Theorem 3.1
.

Given
\theta^{*}\in\mathbb{T},E^{*}\in\mathbb{R}
, we define

S_{0}(\theta^{*},E^{*})=\{x\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}:\ |v(\theta^{*}+x\cdot\omega)-E^{*}|<\delta_{0}\}.

(3.1)

Let
\Lambda\subset\mathbb{Z}^{d}
be a finite set. If
\Lambda\cap S_{0}(\theta^{*},E^{*})=\emptyset
,
then for
|\theta-\theta^{*}|<\delta_{0}/(10D)
,
|E-E^{*}|<\delta_{0}/5
and
\gamma_{0}=\frac{1}{2}|\log\varepsilon|,

\displaystyle\|G_{\Lambda}(\theta,E)\|:=\|\left(H_{\Lambda}(\theta)-E\right)^{-1}\|

\displaystyle\leq 10\delta_{0}^{-1},

\displaystyle|G_{\Lambda}(\theta,E;x,y)|:=|\left(H_{\Lambda}(\theta)-E\right)^{-1}(x,y)|

\displaystyle\leq e^{-\gamma_{0}\|x-y\|_{1}},\ \|x-y\|_{1}\geq 1.

Proof.

For
x\in\Lambda
,
|\theta-\theta^{*}|<\delta_{0}/(10D)
and
|E-E^{*}|<\delta_{0}/5
, one has

|v(\theta+x\cdot\omega)-E|\geq|v(\theta^{*}+x\cdot\omega)-E^{*}|-|E-E^{*}|-D|\theta-\theta^{*}|>\delta_{0}/2.

Thus
V(\theta)
, the diagonal term of
H_{\Lambda}(\theta)
, satisfies
\|(V(\theta)-E)^{-1}\|\leq 2\delta_{0}^{-1}
. Since
\|\Delta\|\leq 2d,\ 4d\varepsilon\delta_{0}^{-1}\leq\frac{1}{2}
, we have by the Neumann series argument

\displaystyle\left(H_{\Lambda}(\theta)-E\right)^{-1}

\displaystyle=\left(\varepsilon\Delta+V(\theta)-E\right)^{-1}

\displaystyle=\sum_{l=0}^{\infty}(-1)^{l}\varepsilon^{l}\left[\left(V(\theta)-E\right)^{-1}\Delta\right]^{l}\left(V(\theta)-E\right)^{-1}.

Thus

\|\left(H_{\Lambda}(\theta)-E\right)^{-1}\|\leq 2\|\left(V(\theta)-E\right)^{-1}\|\leq 4\delta_{0}^{-1}

and

\displaystyle\left|\left(H_{\Lambda}(\theta)-E\right)^{-1}(x,y)\right|

\displaystyle\leq\frac{4}{\delta_{0}}\left(\frac{4d\varepsilon}{\delta_{0}}\right)^{\|x-y\|_{1}}\leq\sqrt{\varepsilon}^{\|x-y\|_{1}}=e^{-\gamma_{0}\|x-y\|_{1}}

provided
\|x-y\|_{1}\geq 1
.
∎

3.2.
The first scale

In this section, we will construct the first generation of Rellich functions
\mathcal{C}_{1}=\mathcal{C}_{1}^{(1)}\cup\mathcal{C}_{1}^{(2)}
to describe the resonance at this scale, where the superscript “
(1)
” indicates the simple resonance case and “
(2)
” the double resonance one.

For this purpose, the first step is to divide the interval
J(v):=\operatorname{Image}v
(i.e., the range of
v
) into simple resonant intervals
\mathcal{J}^{(1)}
and double resonant intervals
\mathcal{J}^{(2)}
. By the assumption of
v
, there are two closed intervals
I_{\pm}
with disjoint interiors, such that

v_{\pm}:=v|_{I\pm},\ \ \pm v^{\prime}_{\pm}\geq 0,\ \ v_{\pm}(I_{\pm})=J(v).

Define

l_{1}^{(1)}:=|\log\varepsilon_{0}|^{4},\ l_{1}^{(2)}:=(l_{1}^{(1)})^{2}\gg 10l_{1}^{(1)}.

Since
v
is monotonic on each
I_{\pm}
, for
0<\|k\|_{1}\leq 10l_{1}^{(1)}
, there is a unique point
\theta_{k,-}\in I_{-}
and its relevant translate
\theta_{k,+}:=\theta_{k,-}+k\cdot\omega
, such that

e_{k}:=v(\theta_{k,-})=v(\theta_{k,+}).

To construct the double resonant intervals
\mathcal{J}^{(2)}
, we need the following lemmas which give the lower bound of
|e_{k}-e_{k^{\prime}}|
for
k\neq k^{\prime}
and
|e_{k}-e_{c}|
, where
e_{c}
is a critical value of
v
.

Lemma 3.2
.

If
\theta_{1},\theta_{2}
belong to the same monotonicity interval
I_{\pm}
, then

|v(\theta_{1})-v(\theta_{2})|\geq\|\theta_{1}-\theta_{2}\|^{2}.

(3.2)

Proof of Lemma
3.2
.

Without loss of generality, we assume
\theta_{1},\theta_{2}\in I_{+}
and
\theta_{1}<\theta_{2}
. By our assumption,
v
is strictly increasing on
I_{+}=[\theta_{m},\theta_{M}]
and satisfies
v^{\prime}(\theta)>2
for
\theta\in[\theta_{m}+a,\theta_{M}-a]
and
v^{\prime\prime}(\theta)>2
(resp.
<2
) for
\theta\in[\theta_{m},\theta_{m}+a]
(resp.
[\theta_{M}-a,\theta_{M}]
).

Case
1:
\theta_{m}\leq\theta_{1}<\theta_{2}\leq\theta_{m}+a
. We have in this case

v(\theta_{2})-v(\theta_{1})=v^{\prime}(\theta_{1})(\theta_{2}-\theta_{1})+\frac{1}{2}v^{\prime\prime}(\xi)(\theta_{2}-\theta_{1})^{2}\geq(\theta_{2}-\theta_{1})^{2}.

Case
2:
\theta_{m}\leq\theta_{1}\leq\theta_{m}+a\leq\theta_{2}\leq\theta_{M}-a
. We have in this case

\displaystyle v(\theta_{2})-v(\theta_{1})

\displaystyle=(v(\theta_{2})-v(a))+(v(a)-v(\theta_{1}))

\displaystyle\geq 2(\theta_{2}-a)+(a-\theta_{1})^{2}

\displaystyle\geq(\theta_{2}-\theta_{1})^{2}.

Case
3:
\theta_{m}+a\leq\theta_{1}<\theta_{2}\leq\theta_{M}-a
. We have

v(\theta_{2})-v(\theta_{1})=v^{\prime}(\xi)(\theta_{2}-\theta_{1})\geq(\theta_{2}-\theta_{1})^{2}.

Case
4:
\theta_{m}<\theta_{1}\leq\theta_{m}+a<\theta_{M}-a\leq\theta_{2}
. We have

v(\theta_{2})-v(\theta_{1})\geq v(\theta_{M}-a)-v(\theta_{m}+a)\geq 2(|I_{+}|-2a)\geq(\theta_{2}-\theta_{1})^{2}.

Case
5:
\theta_{M}-a\leq\theta_{1}<\theta_{2}\leq\theta_{M}
. This case is similar to
Case
1.

Case
6:
\theta_{m}+a\leq\theta_{1}\leq\theta_{M}-a\leq\theta_{2}\leq\theta_{M}
. This case is similar to
Case
2.
∎

Lemma 3.3
.

For
0<\|k\|_{1},\|k^{\prime}\|_{1}\leq 10l_{1}^{(1)}
,
k\neq k^{\prime}
, we have

|e_{k}-e_{k^{\prime}}|\gtrsim{(l_{1}^{(1)})^{-2\tau}}\gg\delta_{0}^{\frac{1}{100}},

(3.3)

|e_{k}-e_{c}|\gtrsim{(l_{1}^{(1)})^{-2\tau}}\gg\delta_{0}^{\frac{1}{100}}.

(3.4)

Proof.

First we prove (
3.3
). By triangle inequality and Diophantine condition, we have

\max(\|\theta_{k,-}-\theta_{k^{\prime},-}\|,\|\theta_{k,+}-\theta_{k^{\prime},+}\|)\geq\|(k-k^{\prime})\cdot\omega\|/2\gtrsim\|k-k^{\prime}\|^{-\tau}\gtrsim(l_{1}^{(1)})^{-\tau}.

If
\|\theta_{k,-}-\theta_{k^{\prime},-}\|\gtrsim(l_{1}^{(1)})^{-\tau}
holds, by Lemma
3.2
we get

|e_{k}-e_{k^{\prime}}|=|v(\theta_{k,-})-v(\theta_{k^{\prime},-})|\geq\|\theta_{k,-}-\theta_{k^{\prime},-}\|^{2}\gtrsim(l_{1}^{(1)})^{-2\tau}.

Likewise, if
\|\theta_{k,+}-\theta_{k^{\prime},+}\|\gtrsim(l_{1}^{(1)})^{-\tau}
holds, we get

|e_{k}-e_{k^{\prime}}|=|v(\theta_{k,+})-v(\theta_{k^{\prime},+})|\geq\|\theta_{k,+}-\theta_{k^{\prime},+}\|^{2}\gtrsim(l_{1}^{(1)})^{-2\tau}.

To see (
3.4
), without loss of generality, we assume
e_{c}=v(\theta_{m})
is the minima of
v
. As above, we have

\max(\|\theta_{k,-}-\theta_{m}\|,\|\theta_{k,+}-\theta_{m}\|)\geq\|(k-k^{\prime})\cdot\omega\|/2\gtrsim\|k-k^{\prime}\|^{-\tau}\gtrsim(l_{1}^{(1)})^{-\tau}.

Thus

|e_{k}-e_{c}|\geq\max(\|\theta_{k,-}-\theta_{m}\|,\|\theta_{k,+}-\theta_{m}\|)^{2}\gtrsim(l_{1}^{(1)})^{-2\tau}.

∎

y
I_{-}
I_{+}
I_{k,-}^{(2)}

I_{k,+}^{(2)}

k\cdot\omega
(\theta_{k,-},e_{k})
J_{k}^{(2)}
k^{\prime}\cdot\omega
(\theta_{k^{\prime},-},e_{k^{\prime}})
v

Figure 2.
Separation of the double resonant intervals and critical values.

Let
B_{\delta}(x_{0})=\{x\in\mathbb{R}:|x-x_{0}|<\delta\}
and let
\bar{B}_{\delta}(x_{0})
be its closure. Define the double resonant intervals

J^{(2)}_{k}:=\bar{B}_{\delta_{0}^{\frac{1}{100}}}(e_{k}),\ 0<\|k\|_{1}\leq 10l_{1}^{(1)}

and the complementary region

J^{SR}:=J\setminus\bigcup_{0<\|k\|\leq 10l_{1}^{(1)}}B_{\delta_{0}^{\frac{1}{100}}-3\delta_{0}}(e_{k}).

(3.5)

Remark 3.1
.

The separation from Lemma
3.3
ensures that
J^{(2)}_{k}\cap J^{(2)}_{k^{\prime}}=\emptyset
for
k\neq k^{\prime}
and the length of each connected component of
J^{SR}
exceeds
\delta_{0}^{\frac{1}{100}}
.

From this observation, we deduce

Proposition 3.4
.

J(v)
can be covered by closed intervals
J_{i}^{(1)}
and
J^{(2)}_{k}
, such that

(1)
.

\delta_{0}^{\frac{1}{100}}\leq|J_{i}^{(1)}|,|J^{(2)}_{k}|\leq 2\delta_{0}^{\frac{1}{100}}
.

(2)
.

J_{i}^{(1)}\subset J^{SR}.

(3)
.

J_{i}^{(1)}\cap J_{i^{\prime}}^{(1)}\neq\emptyset\Rightarrow|J_{i}^{(1)}\cap J_{i^{\prime}}^{(1)}|=3\delta_{0}.

(4)
.

J_{i}^{(1)}\cap J_{k}^{(2)}\neq\emptyset\Rightarrow|J_{i}^{(1)}\cap J_{k}^{(2)}|=3\delta_{0}.

(5)
.

The total number of such intervals does not exceed
2|J(v)|\delta_{0}^{-\frac{1}{100}}
.

Proof.

By Remark
3.1
, we can construct a collection of closed intervals
J_{i}^{(1)}
of size between
\delta_{0}^{\frac{1}{100}}
and
2\delta_{0}^{\frac{1}{100}}
overlapping with only their nearest neighbors by exactly
3\delta_{0}
covering
J^{SR}
. Together with the collection of intervals
J_{k}^{(2)}(0<\|k\|_{1}\leq 10l_{1}^{(1)})
, we have a total of at most
2|J(v)|\delta_{0}^{-\frac{1}{100}}
intervals satisfying the conditions laid
out in the proposition.
∎

We denote by
\mathcal{J}^{(1)}
the collection of simple resonant intervals
J_{i}^{(1)}
and
\mathcal{J}^{(2)}
the collection of double resonant intervals
J_{k}^{(2)}
. The corresponding preimages of
J^{(j)}_{\bullet}
are

I^{(j)}_{\bullet,\pm}:=v_{\pm}^{-1}(J^{(j)}_{\bullet}),\ I^{(j)}_{\bullet}:=I^{(j)}_{\bullet,-}\cup I^{(j)}_{\bullet,+},

where
j\in\{1,2\}
and
\bullet\in\{i,k\}.

By Lemma
3.2
and the uniform bound
|v^{\prime}|\leq D
, we have

|I^{(j)}_{\bullet,\pm}|^{2}\leq|v_{\pm}(I^{(j)}_{\bullet,\pm})|\leq D|I^{(j)}_{\bullet,\pm}|.

Since
|v_{\pm}(I^{(j)}_{\bullet,\pm})|=|J^{(j)}_{\bullet}|\in[\delta_{0}^{\frac{1}{100}},2\delta_{0}^{\frac{1}{100}}]
,
we deduce

\frac{1}{D}\delta_{0}^{\frac{1}{100}}\leq|I^{(j)}_{\bullet,\pm}|\leq 2\delta_{0}^{\frac{1}{200}}.

(3.6)

The remainder of this section is devoted to constructing the
1^{{\rm st}}
generation of Rellich functions
E^{(j)}_{1}
. We do so by case analysis.

3.2.1.
The simple resonance case

In this section, we fix an interval
J_{i}^{(1)}\in\mathcal{J}^{(1)}
and its preimage
I_{i}^{(1)}
.

Proposition 3.5
.

If
\theta\in I^{(1)}_{i}
, then for any
0<\|x\|_{1}\leq l_{1}^{(1)}
, we have

|v(\theta)-v(\theta+x\cdot\omega)|\geq 3\delta_{0}.

Proof.

Suppose
\theta\in I_{i,-}^{(1)}
(the case
\theta\in I_{i,+}^{(1)}
is completely analogous). If
\theta+x\cdot\omega\in I_{-}
, since
0<\|x\|_{1}\leq l_{1}^{(1)}
, we can apply Lemma
3.2
to get

|v(\theta)-v(\theta+x\cdot\omega)|\geq\|x\cdot\omega\|^{2}\gtrsim(l_{1}^{(1)})^{-2\tau}\gg 3\delta_{0}.

We thus suppose
\theta+x\cdot\omega\in I_{+}
.

Assume
\theta\leq\theta_{x,-}
. Then
\theta+x\cdot\omega\leq\theta_{x,+}
. By the monotonicity of
v
on
I_{\pm}
,
v(\theta)\geq e_{x}
and
v(\theta+x\cdot\omega)\leq e_{x}
. Thus by (
3.5
) and item
(2)
of Proposition
3.4
,

v(\theta)-v(\theta+x\cdot\omega)\geq v(\theta)-e_{x}>\delta_{0}^{\frac{1}{100}}-3\delta_{0}>3\delta_{0}.

If
\theta\geq\theta_{x,-}
, then similarly
v(\theta)\leq e_{x}
and
v(\theta+x\cdot\omega)\geq e_{x}
. Thus

|v(\theta)-v(\theta+x\cdot\omega)|=v(\theta+x\cdot\omega)-v(\theta)\geq e_{x}-v(\theta)>3\delta_{0}.

∎

Remark 3.2
.

From Proposition
3.5
it follows that for
\theta\in I^{(1)}_{i},E=v(\theta)
, the origin
o\in S_{0}(\theta,E)
is the only resonant site at
l_{1}^{(1)}
-scale.

For the simple resonance case, we define
B_{1}^{(1)}:=\Lambda_{l_{1}^{(1)}}
. The following proposition shows that the restriction operator
H_{B_{1}^{(1)}}(\theta)
has a unique Rellich function (of order
\delta_{0}
),
E_{1}^{(1)}(\theta)
defined on
I^{(1)}_{i}
near
v(\theta)
.

Proposition 3.6
.

For
\theta\in I^{(1)}_{i}
, we have

(a).

H_{B_{1}^{(1)}}(\theta)
has a unique eigenvalue
E_{1}^{(1)}(\theta)
such that
|E_{1}^{(1)}(\theta)-v(\theta)|\lesssim\varepsilon
. Moreover, any other
\hat{E}\in\sigma(H_{B_{1}^{(1)}}(\theta))
must obey
|\hat{E}-v(\theta)|\geq 2\delta_{0}
.

(b).

The eigenfunction
\psi_{1}
corresponding to
E_{1}^{(1)}
satisfies

|\psi_{1}(x)|\leq e^{-\gamma_{0}\|x\|_{1}}.

(3.7)

(c).

\|G_{1}^{\perp}(E_{1}^{(1)})\|\leq\delta_{0}^{-1}
, where
G_{1}^{\perp}
is the Green’s function for
B_{1}^{(1)}
on the orthogonal complement of
\psi_{1}
.

Proof.

The existence of
E_{1}^{(1)}(\theta)
follows from Lemma
2.2
and the following observation

\|(H_{B_{1}^{(1)}}(\theta)-v(\theta)){\bm{e}}_{o}\|=\|\sum_{\|y\|_{1}=1}\varepsilon{\bm{e}}_{y}\|\lesssim\varepsilon.

Denote
\Lambda=B_{1}^{(1)}\setminus\{o\}
. Let
|E-v(\theta)|\leq 2\delta_{0}
. By Proposition
3.5
,
we have
\Lambda\cap S_{0}(\theta,E)=\emptyset
. Thus by Theorem
3.1
,

|G_{\Lambda}(\theta,E;x,y)|\leq\delta_{0}^{-1}e^{-\gamma_{0}\|x-y\|_{1}}.

Let
E\in\sigma(H_{B_{1}^{(1)}}(\theta))
such that
|E-v(\theta)|\leq 2\delta_{0}
. We determine the value of
\psi_{1}(x)
for
x\neq o
by

\psi_{1}(x)=\sum_{\|y\|_{1}=1}G_{\Lambda}(\theta,E;x,y)\Gamma_{y,o}\psi_{1}\left(o\right).

Thus

|\psi_{1}(x)|\lesssim\frac{\varepsilon}{\delta_{0}}e^{-\gamma_{0}(\|x\|_{1}-1)}\leq e^{-\gamma_{0}\|x\|_{1}}

(3.8)

and we finish the proof of
(b)
.

If there is another
\hat{E}\in\sigma(H_{B_{1}^{(1)}}(\theta))
satisfying
|\hat{E}-v(\theta)|\leq 2\delta_{0}
, its eigenfunction
\hat{\psi}
also satisfies (
3.7
), which violates the orthogonality of
\psi_{1}
and
\hat{\psi}
. Thus we prove the uniqueness part of
(a)
. The item
(c)
follows from any other
E_{1}^{(1)}(\theta)\neq\hat{E}\in\sigma(H_{B_{1}^{(1)}}(\theta))
must obey

|\hat{E}-E_{1}^{(1)}(\theta)|\geq|\hat{E}-v(\theta)|-|v(\theta)-E_{1}^{(1)}(\theta)|\geq 2\delta_{0}-O(\varepsilon)\geq\delta_{0}.

∎

Proposition 3.7
.

We have for
\theta\in I_{i}^{(1)},

|\frac{d^{s}}{d\theta^{s}}(E_{1}^{(1)}(\theta)-v(\theta))|\lesssim\frac{\varepsilon}{\delta_{0}^{s}}\ll\delta_{0}^{10}\ {\rm for\ }s=0,1,2.

Proof.

By the previous proposition, we have

|E_{1}^{(1)}(\theta)-v(\theta)|\lesssim\varepsilon.

(3.9)

Recalling (
3.8
), we have

\|\psi_{1}-\psi_{0}\|\lesssim\frac{\varepsilon}{\delta_{0}}.

(3.10)

For
s=1,2
, we use the Feynman-Hellman formulas
(1)
and
(2)
in Lemma
2.4
. Thus

\displaystyle|(E^{(1)}_{1})^{\prime}(\theta)-v^{\prime}(\theta)|

\displaystyle=|\left\langle\psi_{1},V^{\prime}\psi_{1}\right\rangle-\left\langle\psi_{0},V^{\prime}\psi_{0}\right\rangle|\lesssim\frac{\varepsilon}{\delta_{0}},

\displaystyle(E^{(1)}_{1})^{\prime\prime}(\theta)

\displaystyle=\left\langle\psi_{1},V^{\prime\prime}\psi_{1}\right\rangle-2\left\langle\psi_{1},V^{\prime}G_{1}^{\perp}(E_{1})V^{\prime}\psi_{1}\right\rangle.

Since (
3.10
), we can write

V^{\prime}\psi_{1}=A\psi_{1}+O(\frac{\varepsilon}{\delta_{0}}).

Thus we get

\|P_{1}^{\perp}(V^{\prime}\psi_{1})\|\lesssim\frac{\varepsilon}{\delta_{0}},

where
P_{1}^{\perp}
denotes projection onto the orthogonal complement of
\psi_{1}
. Thus

\displaystyle|(E^{(1)}_{1})^{\prime\prime}(\theta)-v^{\prime\prime}(\theta)|

\displaystyle=|\left\langle\psi_{1},V^{\prime\prime}\psi_{1}\right\rangle-\left\langle\psi_{0},V^{\prime\prime}\psi_{0}\right\rangle-2\left\langle\psi_{1},V^{\prime}G_{1}^{\perp}(E_{1}^{(1)})V^{\prime}\psi_{1}\right\rangle|

\displaystyle\lesssim\frac{\varepsilon}{\delta_{0}}+\|G_{1}^{\perp}(E_{1}^{(1)})\|\cdot\|P_{1}^{\perp}(V^{\prime}\psi_{1})\|

\displaystyle\lesssim\frac{\varepsilon}{\delta_{0}^{2}},

where we have used the estimate from Proposition
3.6

(c)
to bound the term
\|G_{1}^{\perp}(E_{1}^{(1)})\|
.
∎

The next proposition verifies the Morse condition of
E_{1}^{(1)}(\theta)
.

Proposition 3.8
.

Assume
\theta\in I_{i}^{(1)}
such that
|(E^{(1)}_{1})^{\prime}(\theta)|\leq\delta_{0}^{\frac{1}{1000}}
. Then
|(E^{(1)}_{1})^{\prime\prime}(\theta)|\geq 3-\delta_{0}^{10}>2
with a unique sign for all such
\theta
.

Proof.

If
|(E^{(1)}_{1})^{\prime}(\theta)|\leq\delta_{0}^{\frac{1}{1000}}
, then by Proposition
3.7
, we have

|v^{\prime}(\theta)|\leq|(E^{(1)}_{1})^{\prime}(\theta)|+\delta_{0}^{10}\leq 2\delta_{0}^{\frac{1}{1000}}.

By our assumption of
v
(c.f. Remark
2.1
) and since
\delta_{0}^{\frac{1}{1000}}\ll a
, we deduce that

|v^{\prime\prime}(\theta)|\geq 3

with a unique sign for all such
\theta
. Thus by Proposition
3.7
,

|(E^{(1)}_{1})^{\prime\prime}(\theta)|\geq|v^{\prime\prime}(\theta)|-\delta_{0}^{10}\geq 3-\delta_{0}^{10}>2

with a unique sign for all such
\theta
.
∎

At the end of this part, we show each Rellich child
E_{1}^{(1)}
defined on
I^{(1)}_{i}
inherits the two-monotonicity interval structure.
The two-monotonicity interval structure is crucial in the whole induction since it ensures that there are at most two resonant Rellich functions at
each scale. For this purpose, we need a lemma concerning the derivative of
v
.

Lemma 3.9
.

For any
\theta\in\mathbb{T}
, we have

|v^{\prime}(\theta)|\geq\min(\|\theta-\theta_{m}\|,\|\theta-\theta_{M}\|).

Proof.

By the assumption of
v
,
if
\theta\in\{\theta\in\mathbb{T}:\ \|\theta-\theta_{M}\|\geq a\}\cap\{\theta\in\mathbb{T}:\ \|\theta-\theta_{m}\|\geq a\}
, we have

|v^{\prime}(\theta)|\geq 3\geq\min(\|\theta-\theta_{m}\|,\|\theta-\theta_{M}\|).

If
\|\theta-\theta_{M}\|<a
, by the mean value theorem, we have

|v^{\prime}(\theta)|\geq|v^{\prime\prime}(\xi)||\theta-\theta_{M}|\geq\|\theta-\theta_{M}\|.

Likewise, if
\|\theta-\theta_{m}\|<a
, then

|v^{\prime}(\theta)|\geq|v^{\prime\prime}(\xi)||\theta-\theta_{m}|\geq\|\theta-\theta_{m}\|.

∎

Proposition 3.10
.

We have the following:

(a).

Suppose
v
has a critical point in
I^{(1)}_{i}
. (Thus
I^{(1)}_{i}
is a closed interval.) Then
E_{1}^{(1)}
also has a critical point in
I^{(1)}_{i}
. Moreover,
E_{1}^{(1)}
has the two-monotonicity interval structure, that is,
I^{(1)}_{i}
can be divided into two closed intervals
\tilde{I}^{(1)}_{i,+}
and
\tilde{I}^{(1)}_{i,-}
with disjoint interiors, such that

\pm(E^{(1)}_{1})^{\prime}|_{\tilde{I}^{(1)}_{i,\pm}}\geq 0,\ \tilde{I}^{(1)}_{i,+}\cup\tilde{I}^{(1)}_{i,-}=I^{(1)}_{i}.

(b).

Suppose
v
has no critical point in
I^{(1)}_{i}
(Thus
I^{(1)}_{i}=I^{(1)}_{i,+}\cup I^{(1)}_{i,-}
is a union of two closed disjoint intervals.) Then

\pm(E^{(1)}_{1})^{\prime}|_{I^{(1)}_{i,\pm}}\gtrsim\delta_{0}^{\frac{1}{100}}.

Proof.

To prove
(a)
, assume that
\theta_{c}
is the critical point of
v
in
I^{(1)}_{i}
. By (
3.6
), Lemma
3.9
and Proposition
3.7
, we deduce
|(E^{(1)}_{1})^{\prime}|_{\partial I^{(1)}_{i}}|\gtrsim\delta_{0}^{\frac{1}{100}}
with different sign on the edges of
I^{(1)}_{i}
. Since
(E^{(1)}_{1})^{\prime}
is continuous and
I^{(1)}_{i}
is connected, there is point
\theta_{s}\in I^{(1)}_{i}
such that
(E^{(1)}_{1})^{\prime}(\theta_{s})=0
. The two monotonicity structure immediately follows from Proposition
3.8
and Lemma
2.3
.
The uniqueness of
\theta_{s}
on
I_{i}^{(1)}
follows from the two monotonicity structure of
E_{1}^{(1)}
restricted to
I_{i}^{(1)}
.

To prove
(b)
, we notice that by the construction (c.f. Proposition
3.4
), if
J_{i}^{(1)}
does not contain a critical value of
v
, then
J_{i}^{(1)}
has a distance exceeding
\delta_{0}^{\frac{1}{100}}-3\delta_{0}
from the critical value. Thus its preimage
I_{i}^{(1)}
has a distance on order
\delta_{0}^{\frac{1}{100}}
from the critical point. By Lemma
3.9
, we get

\pm v|_{I^{(1)}_{i,\pm}}\gtrsim\delta_{0}^{\frac{1}{100}}.

The desired estimate for
\pm(E^{(1)}_{1})^{\prime}|_{I^{(1)}_{i,\pm}}
follows from the above estimate and Proposition
3.7
immediately.
∎

We finish the proof in the simple resonance case and consider the double resonance case in the next part.

3.2.2.
The double resonance case

In this section, we fix
J_{k}^{(2)}\in\mathcal{J}^{(2)}\ (0<\|k\|_{1}\leq 10l_{1}^{(1)})
and define the domain

I^{(2)}_{k,\cup}:=I^{(2)}_{k,-}\cup(I^{(2)}_{k,+}-k\cdot\omega),

which is a single interval with
\delta_{0}^{\frac{1}{100}}\lesssim|I^{(2)}_{k,\cup}|\leq 4\delta_{0}^{\frac{1}{200}}
and
\operatorname{dist}(k,\partial I^{(2)}_{k,\cup})\gtrsim\delta_{0}^{\frac{1}{100}}
.

Proposition 3.11
.

If
\theta\in I^{(2)}_{k,\cup}
, then for any
\|x\|_{1}\leq(l_{1}^{(1)})^{4}
,
x\notin\{o,k\}
, we have

|v(\theta)-v(\theta+x\cdot\omega)|\geq 2\delta_{0}^{\frac{1}{400}}.

Proof.

We first consider the case that
\theta^{*}=\theta_{k,-}
. If
\theta^{*}+x\cdot\omega\in I_{-}
, then by Lemma
3.2
and Diophantine condition, we have

|v(\theta^{*})-v(\theta^{*}+x\cdot\omega)|\geq\|x\cdot\omega\|^{2}\gtrsim(l_{1}^{(1)})^{-8\tau}\gg\delta_{0}^{\frac{1}{400}}.

If instead
\theta^{*}+x\cdot\omega\in I_{+}
, then

|v(\theta^{*})-v(\theta^{*}+x\cdot\omega)|=|v(\theta^{*}+k\cdot\omega)-v(\theta^{*}+x\cdot\omega)|\geq\|(k-x)\cdot\omega\|^{2}\gtrsim(l_{1}^{(1)})^{-8\tau}\gg\delta_{0}^{\frac{1}{400}}.

The claim for
\theta\in I^{(2)}_{k,\cup}
follows from the mean value theorem, (
3.6
) and the uniform bound
|v^{\prime}|\leq D
.
∎

Remark 3.3
.

Proposition
3.11
ensures that for
\theta\in I^{(2)}_{k,\cup},E=v(\theta)
, the origin
o
and
k
are the only resonant sites at
(l_{1}^{(1)})^{4}
-scale.

For the double resonance case, we define
B_{1}^{(2)}:=\Lambda_{l_{1}^{(2)}}
. The following proposition shows that the restriction operator
H_{B_{1}^{(2)}}(\theta)
has only two Rellich functions (of order
\delta_{0}^{\frac{1}{400}}
),
E_{1}^{(2)}(\theta)
and
\mathcal{E}_{1}^{(2)}(\theta)
defined on
I^{(2)}_{k,\cup}
near
v(\theta)
.

Proposition 3.12
.

The following holds for
\theta\in I^{(2)}_{k,\cup}
:

(a).

H_{B_{1}^{(2)}}(\theta)
has exactly two eigenvalues
E_{1}^{(2)}(\theta)
and
\mathcal{E}_{1}^{(2)}(\theta)
in
[v(\theta)-5D\delta_{0}^{\frac{1}{200}},v(\theta)+5D\delta_{0}^{\frac{1}{200}}]
. Moreover, any other
\hat{E}\in\sigma(H_{B_{1}^{(2)}}(\theta))
must obey
|\hat{E}-v(\theta)|\geq 2\delta_{0}^{\frac{1}{400}}
.

(b).

The eigenfunction
\psi_{1}

(resp.
\Psi_{1}
)
corresponding to
E_{1}^{(2)}

(resp.
\mathcal{E}_{1}^{(2)}
)
decays exponentially fast away from
o
and
k
, i.e.,

|\psi_{1}(x)|\leq e^{-\gamma_{0}\|x\|_{1}}+e^{-\gamma_{0}\|x-k\|_{1}},

|\Psi_{1}(x)|\leq e^{-\gamma_{0}\|x\|_{1}}+e^{-\gamma_{0}\|x-k\|_{1}}.

Moreover, the two eigenfunctions can be expressed as

\displaystyle\begin{split}\psi_{1}=A\delta(x-o)+B\delta(x-k)+O(\delta_{0}^{10}),\\
\Psi_{1}=B\delta(x-o)-A\delta(x-k)+O(\delta_{0}^{10}),\end{split}

(3.11)

where
A,B
depend on
\theta
satisfying
A^{2}+B^{2}=1
.

(c).

\|G_{1}^{\perp\perp}(E_{1}^{(2)})\|,\|G_{1}^{\perp\perp}(\mathcal{E}_{1}^{(2)})\|\leq\delta_{0}^{-\frac{1}{400}}
, where
G_{1}^{\perp\perp}
denotes the Green’s function for
B_{1}^{(2)}
on the orthogonal complement of the space spanned by
\psi_{1}
and
\Psi_{1}
.

Proof.

We first consider the case
\theta^{*}=\theta_{k,-}
. In this case, since
v(\theta^{*})=v(\theta^{*}+k\cdot\omega)
,
\delta(x-o)
and
\delta(x-k)
serve as two trial wave functions for the operator
H_{B_{1}^{(2)}}(\theta^{*})-v(\theta^{*})
. By Lemma
2.2
, we deduce
H_{B_{1}^{(2)}}(\theta^{*})
has two eigenvalues in
[v(\theta^{*})-O(\varepsilon),v(\theta^{*})+O(\varepsilon)]
. The claim for
\theta\in I^{(2)}_{k,\cup}
follows from
|I^{(2)}_{k,\cup}|\leq 4\delta_{0}^{\frac{1}{200}}
and the uniform bound
|v^{\prime}|\leq D
.

To establish the decay of eigenfunctions, we denote
\Lambda=B^{(2)}_{1}\setminus\{o,k\}
. By Proposition
3.11
, for
x\in\Lambda
,

|E_{1}^{(2)}(\theta)-v(\theta+x\cdot\omega)|\geq|v(\theta)-v(\theta+x\cdot\omega)|-|E_{1}^{(2)}(\theta)-v(\theta)|\geq 2\delta_{0}^{\frac{1}{400}}-O(\varepsilon)>\delta_{0}^{\frac{1}{400}}.

By Neumann series argument, we get

\|G_{\Lambda}(E_{1}^{(2)})\|\leq\delta_{0}^{-\frac{1}{400}},\ |G_{\Lambda}(E_{1}^{(2)};x,y)|\leq\delta_{0}^{-\frac{1}{400}}e^{-\gamma_{0}\|x-y\|_{1}},

which together with restricting the equation
(H_{B_{1}^{(2)}}-E_{1}^{(2)})\psi_{1}=0
to
\Lambda
yields the exponential decay of
\psi_{1}
. The proof of exponential decay of
\Psi_{1}
is analogous.
The expression (
3.11
) follows from the above estimates and the orthogonality of
\psi_{1}
and
\Psi_{1}
.

If there is a third eigenvalue
|\hat{E}-v(\theta)|<2\delta_{0}^{\frac{1}{400}}
, the above argument shows that its eigenfunction can also be expressed as the form (
3.11
). This violates the orthogonality relation of
\psi_{1},\Psi_{1}
and leads to the contradiction.

Item
(c)
follows from
(a)
immediately.
∎

Remark 3.4
.

Thanks to the perturbation theory for self-adjoint operators, the two Rellich functions can be properly parameterized so that
E_{1}^{(2)}(\theta),\mathcal{E}_{1}^{(2)}(\theta)\in C^{1}(I^{(2)}_{k,\cup})
, although the level crossing issue may occur. We will use the
C^{1}
-parameterization for
E_{1}^{(2)}
and
\mathcal{E}_{1}^{(2)}
in the following discussion.

Verifying the Morse condition of
E_{1}^{(2)}(\theta)
and
\mathcal{E}_{1}^{(2)}(\theta)
in double resonance case needs more efforts.

For conciseness, we define

E_{0,-}(\theta)=v(\theta),\ E_{0,+}(\theta)=v(\theta+k\cdot\omega),

\psi_{0,-}:=\delta(x-o),\ \psi_{0,+}:=\delta(x-k),

and

E_{1,>}^{(2)}(\theta)=\max(E_{1}^{(2)}(\theta),\mathcal{E}_{1}^{(2)}(\theta)),\ E_{1,<}^{(2)}(\theta)=\min(E_{1}^{(2)}(\theta),\mathcal{E}_{1}^{(2)}(\theta)).

Lemma 3.13
.

For
\theta\in I_{k,\cup}^{(2)}
, we have

\pm E_{0,\pm}^{\prime}(\theta)\gtrsim(l_{1}^{(1)})^{-2\tau}.

Proof.

Recalling (
3.4
) and
|v^{\prime}|\leq D
, we have

\|\theta_{k,\pm}-\theta_{m}\|,\|\theta_{k,\pm}-\theta_{M}\|\gtrsim(l_{1}^{(1)})^{-2\tau}.

Since
\theta\in I_{k,\cup}^{(2)}
and
|I_{k,\cup}^{(2)}|\leq 4\delta_{0}^{\frac{1}{200}}\ll(l_{1}^{(1)})^{-2\tau}
, we have

\|\theta-\theta_{m}\|,\|\theta-\theta_{M}\|,\|\theta+k\cdot\omega-\theta_{m}\|,\|\theta+k\cdot\omega-\theta_{M}\|\gtrsim(l_{1}^{(1)})^{-2\tau}.

The above estimates together with Lemma
3.9
yield the desired lower bound.
∎

Theorem 3.14

(Cauchy Interlacing Theorem)
.

Let
A
be a self-adjoint operator on a Hilbert space of dimension
n
, let
m\leqslant n
, and let
P
be an
m
-dimensional orthogonal projection. Let
B=PAP|_{\operatorname{Image}P}
be a compression of
A
, and denote the (ordered) eigenvalues of
A
(resp.
B
) by
\alpha_{1}\leqslant\alpha_{2}\leqslant\cdots\leqslant\alpha_{n}
(resp.
\beta_{1}\leqslant\beta_{2}\leqslant\cdots\leqslant\beta_{m}
). Then

\alpha_{k}\leqslant\beta_{k}\leqslant\alpha_{k+n-m}.

Proof.

The interlacing theorem is proven via a standard min-max argument.
∎

To settle the level crossing issue, we use Theorem
3.14
to construct a pair of auxiliary functions
\lambda_{\pm}
. For our purpose, let

P_{\pm}:=\langle\psi_{0,\pm}|\psi_{0,\pm}\rangle,\ Q_{\pm}:=\operatorname{Id}_{B_{1}^{(2)}}-P_{\pm},

and consider the compressed operators

H_{\pm}(\theta):=Q_{\pm}H_{B_{1}^{(2)}}(\theta)Q_{\pm}.

Lemma 3.15
.

For
\theta\in I^{(2)}_{k,\cup}
, the operators
H_{\pm}(\theta)
have unique eigenvalues
\lambda_{\mp}(\theta)
interlacing
E_{1,>}^{(2)}(\theta)
and
E_{1,<}^{(2)}(\theta)
:

E_{1,<}^{(2)}(\theta)\leq\lambda_{\mp}(\theta)\leq E_{1,>}^{(2)}(\theta).

(3.12)

Moreover,
\lambda_{\pm}(\theta)
are
C^{1}
-closed to
E_{0,\pm}(\theta)
:

|\frac{d^{s}}{d\theta^{s}}(\lambda_{\pm}(\theta)-E_{0,\pm}(\theta))|\lesssim\frac{\varepsilon}{\delta_{0}^{s}}\ll\delta_{0}^{10},\ s=0,1.

(3.13)

In particular, we have

\pm\lambda_{\pm}^{\prime}(\theta)\gtrsim(l_{1}^{(1)})^{-2\tau}\gg\delta_{0}^{\frac{1}{1000}}.

(3.14)

Proof.

We claim that
H_{\pm}(\theta)
has a unique eigenvalue
\lambda_{\mp}(\theta)
in
[v(\theta)-\delta_{0}^{\frac{1}{400}},v(\theta)+\delta_{0}^{\frac{1}{400}}]
satisfying

|\lambda_{\pm}(\theta)-E_{0,\pm}(\theta)|\lesssim\varepsilon.

For the existence, we notice that

\|(H_{\pm}(\theta)-E_{0,\mp}(\theta))\psi_{0,\mp}\|=\|Q_{\pm}(H(\theta)-E_{0,\mp}(\theta))\psi_{0,\mp}\|\lesssim\varepsilon

and employ Lemma
2.2
to finish the proof.

For the uniqueness, assuming
\hat{\lambda}
is an eigenvalue of
H_{\pm}(\theta)
satisfying
|\hat{\lambda}-v(\theta)|\leq\delta_{0}^{\frac{1}{400}}
, then its eigenfunction
\hat{\phi}\in\operatorname{Image}Q_{\pm}
satisfies

Q_{\pm}(H_{B_{1}^{(2)}}(\theta)-\hat{\lambda})\hat{\phi}=0.

Thus

(H_{B_{1}^{(2)}}(\theta)-\hat{\lambda})\hat{\phi}=a\psi_{0,\pm}.

By the same argument as the proof of item
(b)
of Proposition
3.12
, one can prove that
\hat{\phi}=\psi_{0,\mp}+O(\varepsilon\delta_{0}^{-1})
since
\hat{\phi}\in\operatorname{Image}Q_{\pm}
. Thus such an eigenvalue must be unique or else it will violate the orthogonality. By Theorem
3.14
and item
(a)
of Proposition
3.12
,
\lambda_{\mp}(\theta)
must lie between
E_{1,<}^{(2)}(\theta)
and
E_{1,>}^{(2)}(\theta)
.
Let
\phi_{\mp}\in\operatorname{Image}Q_{\pm}
be the eigenfunctions of
H_{\pm}
corresponding to
\lambda_{\mp}
. Since
\|\phi_{\mp}-\psi_{0,\mp}\|\lesssim\varepsilon\delta_{0}^{-1}
, by the Feynman-Hellman formula
(1)
from Lemma
2.4
, we have

\displaystyle|\lambda_{\mp}^{\prime}-E_{0,\mp}^{\prime}|

\displaystyle=|\left\langle\phi_{\mp},(H_{\pm})^{\prime}\phi_{\mp}\right\rangle-\left\langle\psi_{0,\mp},H^{\prime}\psi_{0,\mp}\right\rangle|

\displaystyle=|\left\langle\phi_{\mp},V^{\prime}\phi_{\mp}\right\rangle-\left\langle\psi_{0,\mp},V^{\prime}\psi_{0,\mp}\right\rangle|

\displaystyle\lesssim\frac{\varepsilon}{\delta_{0}}\ll\delta_{0}^{10}.

Finally, (
3.14
) follows from the above inequality and Lemma
3.13
.
∎

Remark 3.5
.

The interlacing argument (c.f. Theorem
3.14
and Lemma
3.15
) was first introduced by Forman-VandenBoom
[
FV21
]
to study a pair of double resonant Rellich functions. In the one-dimensional case, the interlacing argument together with the classical eigenvalue separation lemma for Jacobi matrix (c.f.
[
FV21
]
, Lemma 1.5) provides a uniform gap of the two Rellich graphs in the vertical direction. Since the gap is much larger than the next resonance strength, energies near the codomain of one resonant Rellich function avoid the resonance with the other one, crucially enabling their induction. The present work extends the interlacing idea to the multi-dimensional setting, where the level crossing issue of a pair of double resonant Rellich functions may present. We introduce a new type of Rellich functions (c.f.
Type

3
) to handle this issue and enable our induction.

Lemma 3.16
.

There is a unique point
\theta_{s}\in I^{(2)}_{k,\cup}
satisfying
|\theta_{s}-\theta_{k,-}|<\delta_{0}^{10}
, such that

\lambda_{+}(\theta_{s})=\lambda_{-}(\theta_{s}).

Moreover, the Rellich children have quantitative separation away from
\theta_{s}
:

E_{1,>}^{(2)}(\theta)-E_{1,<}^{(2)}(\theta)\geq|\lambda_{+}(\theta)-\lambda_{-}(\theta)|\geq\delta_{0}^{\frac{1}{1000}}|\theta-\theta_{s}|.

(3.15)

As a corollary, if
\theta\in I^{(2)}_{k,\cup}
satisfies
E_{1}^{(2)}(\theta)=\mathcal{E}_{1}^{(2)}(\theta)
, then
\theta=\theta_{s}
.

Proof.

We consider the difference function

d(\theta):=\lambda_{+}(\theta)-\lambda_{-}(\theta).

Since
E_{0,+}(\theta_{k,-})=E_{0,-}(\theta_{k,-})
, by (
3.13
) (the version
s=0
), we have

|d(\theta_{k,-})|\leq|\lambda_{+}(\theta_{k,-})-E_{0,+}(\theta_{k,-})|+|\lambda_{-}(\theta_{k,-})-E_{0,-}(\theta_{k,-})|\lesssim\varepsilon.

Recalling (
3.14
), we have

d^{\prime}(\theta)\geq\delta_{0}^{\frac{1}{1000}}.

Thus by mean value theorem there exists a unique point
\theta_{s}
satisfying
|\theta_{s}-\theta_{k,-}|\lesssim\varepsilon\delta_{0}^{-\frac{1}{1000}}<\delta_{0}^{10}
, such that

d(\theta_{s})=0
, that is

\lambda_{+}(\theta_{s})=\lambda_{-}(\theta_{s}).

Finally, (
3.15
) follows from (
3.12
) and

|\lambda_{+}(\theta)-\lambda_{-}(\theta)|=|d(\theta)|=|d(\theta)-d(\theta_{s})|\geq\delta_{0}^{\frac{1}{1000}}|\theta-\theta_{s}|

using mean value theorem.
∎

\theta_{s}
I_{k,\cup}^{(2)}
\lambda_{+}
E_{1,>}^{(2)}
\lambda_{-}
E_{1,<}^{(2)}
\theta_{s}
I_{k,\cup}^{(2)}
\lambda_{+}
E_{1,>}^{(2)}
\lambda_{-}
E_{1,<}^{(2)}

Figure 3.
A cartoon illustration of Lemma
3.15
: the graph on the left shows the eigenvalue separation case and the graph on the right shows the level crossing case.

Remark 3.6
.

In the following, we assume that the Rellich functions are chosen so that
E_{1}^{(2)}>\mathcal{E}_{1}^{(2)}
on the right of
\theta_{s}
.

Suppose that
|E_{0,+}^{\prime}(\theta_{k,-})|\geq|E_{0,-}^{\prime}(\theta_{k,-})|
(the argument is similar to the opposite case). Recalling Lemma
3.13
, we define
1\leq r\lesssim(l_{1}^{(1)})^{2\tau}
such that

|E_{0,+}^{\prime}(\theta_{k,-})|=r|E_{0,-}^{\prime}(\theta_{k,-})|.

Lemma 3.17
.

For
\theta\in I_{k,\cup}^{(2)}
, we have

|(E_{0,+}^{\prime}+rE_{0,-}^{\prime})(\theta)|\leq\delta_{0}^{\frac{1}{400}}.

Proof.

Since
E_{0,+}^{\prime}
and
E_{0,-}^{\prime}
have opposite signs, we have

E_{0,+}^{\prime}(\theta_{k,-})+rE_{0,-}^{\prime}(\theta_{k,-})=0.

By mean value theorem and
|E_{0,\pm}^{\prime\prime}|\leq D
, we have

|(E_{0,+}^{\prime}+rE_{0,-}^{\prime})(\theta)|\leq(1+r)D|\theta-\theta_{k,-}|\leq(1+r)D\delta_{0}^{\frac{1}{200}}\leq\delta_{0}^{\frac{1}{400}}.

∎

Proposition 3.18
.

For
\theta\in I_{k,\cup}^{(2)}
, we have the following:

(a).

If
E_{1}^{(2)}(\theta)\neq\mathcal{E}_{1}^{(2)}(\theta)
, then

\displaystyle(E_{1}^{(2)})^{\prime}

\displaystyle=(A^{2}-rB^{2})E_{0,-}^{\prime}+O(\delta_{0}^{\frac{1}{400}}),

(3.16)

\displaystyle(\mathcal{E}_{1}^{(2)})^{\prime}

\displaystyle=(B^{2}-rA^{2})E_{0,-}^{\prime}+O(\delta_{0}^{\frac{1}{400}}),

(3.17)

and

\displaystyle(E_{1}^{(2)})^{\prime\prime}

\displaystyle=\frac{2\left\langle\psi_{1},V^{\prime}\Psi_{1}\right\rangle^{2}}{E_{1}^{(2)}-\mathcal{E}_{1}^{(2)}}+O(\delta_{0}^{-\frac{1}{400}}),

(3.18)

\displaystyle(\mathcal{E}_{1}^{(2)})^{\prime\prime}

\displaystyle=\frac{2\left\langle\psi_{1},V^{\prime}\Psi_{1}\right\rangle^{2}}{\mathcal{E}_{1}^{(2)}-E_{1}^{(2)}}+O(\delta_{0}^{-\frac{1}{400}})

(3.19)

with the notation from Proposition
3.12
and
r
as above.

(b).

If
E_{1}^{(2)}(\theta)\neq\mathcal{E}_{1}^{(2)}(\theta)
and
|(E_{1}^{(2)})^{\prime}(\theta)|\leq\delta_{0}^{\frac{1}{1000}}
, then
|(E_{1}^{(2)})^{\prime\prime}(\theta)|\geq 2
. Moreover, the sign of
(E_{1}^{(2)})^{\prime\prime}(\theta)
is the same as that of
E_{1}^{(2)}(\theta)-\mathcal{E}_{1}^{(2)}(\theta)
. The analogous conclusion holds if we exchange
E_{1}^{(2)}(\theta)
and
\mathcal{E}_{1}^{(2)}(\theta)
.

(c).

If there is a level crossing (thus
E_{1}^{(2)}(\theta_{s})=\mathcal{E}_{1}^{(2)}(\theta_{s})
by Lemma
3.16
), then for all
\theta\in I_{k,\cup}^{(2)}
,

(E_{1}^{(2)})^{\prime}(\theta)>\delta_{0}^{\frac{1}{1000}},\ (\mathcal{E}_{1}^{(2)})^{\prime}(\theta)<-\delta_{0}^{\frac{1}{1000}}.

(3.20)

Proof.

We prove (
3.16
) (the proof of (
3.17
) is analogous). By (
3.11
) and Lemma
3.17
, we apply Feynman-Hellman formula
(1)
in Lemma
2.4
to obtain

\displaystyle(E_{1}^{(2)})^{\prime}

\displaystyle=\left\langle\psi_{1},V^{\prime}\psi_{1}\right\rangle=A^{2}\left\langle\psi_{0,-},V^{\prime}\psi_{0,-}\right\rangle+B^{2}\left\langle\psi_{0,+},V^{\prime}\psi_{0,+}\right\rangle+O(\delta_{0}^{10})

\displaystyle=A^{2}E_{0,-}^{\prime}+B^{2}E_{0,+}^{\prime}+O(\delta_{0}^{10})

\displaystyle=(A^{2}-rB^{2})E_{0,-}^{\prime}+B^{2}(E_{0,+}^{\prime}+rE_{0,-}^{\prime})+O(\delta_{0}^{10})

\displaystyle=(A^{2}-rB^{2})E_{0,-}^{\prime}+O(\delta_{0}^{\frac{1}{400}}).

To prove (
3.18
) and (
3.19
), we use Feynman-Hellman formulas
(2)
and
(3)
to obtain

(E_{1}^{(2)})^{\prime\prime}=\left\langle\psi_{1},V^{\prime\prime}\psi_{1}\right\rangle+2\frac{\left\langle\psi_{1},V^{\prime}\Psi_{1}\right\rangle^{2}}{E_{1}^{(2)}-\mathcal{E}_{1}^{(2)}}-2\left\langle V^{\prime}\psi_{1},G^{\perp\perp}_{1}(E_{1}^{(2)})V^{\prime}\psi_{1}\right\rangle.

The first term is bounded by
D
and the third term is bounded by

2\|G^{\perp\perp}_{1}(E_{1}^{(2)})\|\cdot\|V^{\prime}\psi_{1}\|^{2}\lesssim\delta_{0}^{-\frac{1}{400}},

where we use the estimate
\|G^{\perp\perp}_{1}(E_{1}^{(2)})\|\leq\delta_{0}^{-\frac{1}{400}}
from item
(c)
of Proposition
3.12
. Thus we finish the proof of item
(a)
.

Now we are going to prove
(b)
. We will show the first term in (
3.18
) is large if
|(E_{1}^{(2)})^{\prime}(\theta)|\leq\delta_{0}^{\frac{1}{1000}}
.
Assume
|(E_{1}^{(2)})^{\prime}(\theta)|\leq\delta_{0}^{\frac{1}{1000}}
, by (
3.16
), we have

|A^{2}-rB^{2}|\cdot|E_{0,-}^{\prime}(\theta)|\leq|(E_{1}^{(2)})^{\prime}(\theta)|+O(\delta_{0}^{\frac{1}{400}})\leq 2\delta_{0}^{\frac{1}{1000}}.

From Lemma
3.13
, it follows that

|A^{2}-rB^{2}|\leq 2\delta_{0}^{\frac{1}{1000}}(l_{1}^{(1)})^{2\tau}<\frac{1}{100}.

Since
A^{2}+B^{2}=1
and
r\geq 1
, we obtain

(1+r)B^{2}\geq A^{2}+B^{2}-|A^{2}-rB^{2}|\geq\frac{99}{100}

and

(1+\frac{1}{r})A^{2}\geq A^{2}+B^{2}-\frac{1}{r}|A^{2}-rB^{2}|\geq\frac{99}{100}.

Thus

B^{2}\geq\frac{1}{4r},\ A^{2}\geq\frac{1}{4}.

By (
3.11
), Lemmas
3.13
,
3.17
and the lower bounds on
A,B
, we have

\displaystyle|\langle\psi_{1},V^{\prime}\Psi_{1}\rangle|

\displaystyle=|AB(\langle\psi_{0,-},V^{\prime}\psi_{0,-}\rangle-\langle\psi_{0,+},V^{\prime}\psi_{0,+}\rangle)+O(\delta_{0}^{10})|

\displaystyle=|AB(E_{0,-}^{\prime}-E_{0,+}^{\prime})+O(\delta_{0}^{10})|

\displaystyle=|AB\left((1+r)E_{0,-}^{\prime}-(E_{0,+}^{\prime}+rE_{0,-}^{\prime})\right)+O(\delta_{0}^{10})|

\displaystyle=\frac{1+r}{4\sqrt{r}}|E_{0,-}^{\prime}|-O(\delta_{0}^{\frac{1}{400}})

\displaystyle\gtrsim(l_{1}^{(1)})^{-2\tau}\gg\delta_{0}^{\frac{1}{1000}}.

By item
(a)
of Proposition
3.12
, we obtain the estimate of the denominator
|E_{1}^{(2)}-\mathcal{E}_{1}^{(2)}|\lesssim\delta_{0}^{\frac{1}{200}}
. Combining the previous estimate of numerator, by (
3.18
), we obtain

|(E_{1}^{(2)})^{\prime\prime}(\theta)|\gtrsim\delta_{0}^{\frac{1}{500}}\delta_{0}^{-\frac{1}{200}}-O(\delta_{0}^{-\frac{1}{400}})>2,

whose sign is the same as that of
E_{1}^{(2)}(\theta)-\mathcal{E}_{1}^{(2)}(\theta)
.

To prove
(c)
, we first show (
3.20
) holds for
\theta=\theta_{s}
. Since
E_{1}^{(2)}(\theta_{s})=\mathcal{E}_{1}^{(2)}(\theta_{s})
and (
3.12
), we have

E_{1}^{(2)}(\theta_{s})=\mathcal{E}_{1}^{(2)}(\theta_{s})=\lambda_{+}(\theta_{s})=\lambda_{-}(\theta_{s}),

and since
E_{1}^{(2)}>\mathcal{E}_{1}^{(2)}
on the right of
\theta_{s}
(c.f. Remark
3.6
), we have for
\theta>\theta_{s}
,

E_{1}^{(2)}(\theta)\geq\lambda_{+}(\theta)>\lambda_{-}(\theta)\geq\mathcal{E}_{1}^{(2)}(\theta).

Thus by (
3.14
),
we get

(E_{1}^{(2)})^{\prime}(\theta_{s})\geq\lambda_{+}^{\prime}(\theta_{s})>\delta_{0}^{\frac{1}{1000}},\ (\mathcal{E}_{1}^{(2)})^{\prime}(\theta_{s})\leq\lambda_{-}^{\prime}(\theta_{s})<-\delta_{0}^{\frac{1}{1000}}.

We next claim the inequalities hold for all
\theta\in I_{k,\cup}^{(2)}
. We prove the case that
(E_{1}^{(2)})^{\prime}(\theta)
and
\theta>\theta_{s}
(the other cases are analogous). If it is not true, then the set

\{\theta\in I_{k,\cup}^{(2)}:\ \theta>\theta_{s},\ \ (E_{1}^{(2)})^{\prime}(\theta)\leq\delta_{0}^{\frac{1}{1000}}\}\neq\emptyset.

Let
\theta^{*}
be its infimum. Since
E_{1}^{\prime}(\theta)
is continuous and
(E_{1}^{(2)})^{\prime}(\theta_{s})>\delta_{0}^{\frac{1}{1000}}
, we have
\theta^{*}>\theta_{s}
and
(E_{1}^{(2)})^{\prime}(\theta)>\delta_{0}^{\frac{1}{1000}}\geq(E_{1}^{(2)})^{\prime}(\theta^{*})
for
\theta\in[\theta_{s},\theta^{*})
, which implies
(E_{1}^{(2)})^{\prime\prime}(\theta^{*})\leq 0
. However, by item
(b)
and
E_{1}^{(2)}(\theta^{*})>\mathcal{E}_{1}^{(2)}(\theta^{*})
, we get
(E_{1}^{(2)})^{\prime\prime}(\theta^{*})>2
, a contradiction. Thus we finish the proof of the claim.
∎

The following proposition shows that
E_{1,<}^{(2)}(\theta)
and
E_{1,>}^{(2)}(\theta)
are uniformly closed (of order
\varepsilon
) to the previous scale functions
E_{0,\pm}(\theta)
, providing a more completed picture for the structure of the Rellich functions.

Define

E_{0,\vee}(\theta):=\max(E_{0,+}(\theta),E_{0,-}(\theta)),\ E_{0,\wedge}(\theta):=\min(E_{0,+}(\theta),E_{0,-}(\theta)).

Proposition 3.19
.

For
\theta\in I_{k,\cup}^{(2)}
, we have

|E_{1,>}^{(2)}(\theta)-E_{0,\vee}(\theta)|,|E_{1,<}^{(2)}(\theta)-E_{0,\wedge}(\theta)|\lesssim\varepsilon.

(3.21)

Proof.

Fix
\theta\in I_{k,\cup}^{(2)}
. We have

\|(H_{B_{1}^{(2)}}-E_{0,\pm})\psi_{0,\pm}\|\lesssim\varepsilon.

By Lemma
2.2
, we deduce
H_{B_{1}^{(2)}}
must have an eigenvalue in
[E_{0,\vee}-O(\varepsilon),E_{0,\vee}+O(\varepsilon)]
and in
[E_{0,\wedge}-O(\varepsilon),E_{0,\wedge}+O(\varepsilon)]
. By item
(a)
of proposition
3.12
, the eigenvalue must be
E_{1}^{(2)}
or
\mathcal{E}_{1}^{(2)}
. If the two intervals are disjoint, (
3.21
) must hold.
Otherwise, we have

E_{0,\vee}-E_{0,\wedge}\lesssim\varepsilon.

(3.22)

Thus we have two trial functions for
E_{0,\vee}
:

\|(H_{B_{1}^{(2)}}-E_{0,\vee})\psi_{0,\pm}\|\lesssim\varepsilon.

By Lemma
2.2
,
H_{B_{1}^{(2)}}
must have two eigenvalues in
[E_{0,\vee}-O(\varepsilon),E_{0,\vee}+O(\varepsilon)].
They are
E_{1}^{(2)}
and
\mathcal{E}_{1}^{(2)}
. Combining (
3.22
) shows (
3.21
).
∎

At the end of this part, we show that the two Rellich children
E_{1,>}^{(2)}
and
E_{1,<}^{(2)}
have the two-monotonicity interval structure.

Proposition 3.20
.

We have the following:

(a).

If there is no level crossing, then
E_{1}^{(2)}=E_{1,>}^{(2)}
and
\mathcal{E}_{1}^{(2)}=E_{1,<}^{(2)}
. Moreover,
E_{1}^{(2)}
(resp.
\mathcal{E}_{1}^{(2)}
) has the two-monotonicity interval structure with a critical point
\theta_{>}
(resp.
\theta_{<}
) satisfying
|\theta_{>}-\theta_{k,-}|<\delta_{0}^{10}
(resp.
|\theta_{<}-\theta_{k,-}|<\delta_{0}^{10}
).

(b).

If there is a level crossing (thus
E_{1}^{(2)}(\theta_{s})=\mathcal{E}_{1}^{(2)}(\theta_{s})
by Lemma
3.16
), then

\displaystyle E_{1}^{(2)}(\theta)=\left\{\begin{aligned} &E_{1,<}^{(2)}(\theta)&\text{if $\theta<\theta_{s}$,}\\
&E_{1,>}^{(2)}(\theta)&\text{if $\theta>\theta_{s}$,}\end{aligned}\right.

and

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}_{1}^{(2)}(\theta)=\left\{\begin{aligned} &E_{1,>}^{(2)}(\theta)&\text{if $\theta<\theta_{s}$,}\\
&E_{1,<}^{(2)}(\theta)&\text{if $\theta>\theta_{s}$.}\end{aligned}\right.

Moreover,
E_{1,>}^{(2)}
and
E_{1,<}^{(2)}
are piecewise
C^{1}
functions (except at the point
\theta_{s}
) with two-monotonicity interval structure.

Proof.

If there is no level crossing, then
E_{1,>}^{(2)}(\theta)>E_{1,<}^{(2)}(\theta)
for all
\theta\in I_{k,\cup}^{(2)}
. Since
E_{1}^{(2)}>\mathcal{E}_{1}^{(2)}
on the right of
\theta_{s}
, by continuity
E_{1}^{(2)}(\theta)>\mathcal{E}_{1}^{(2)}(\theta)
for all
\theta\in I_{k,\cup}^{(2)}
. Thus
E_{1}^{(2)}=E_{1,>}^{(2)}
and
\mathcal{E}_{1}^{(2)}=E_{1,<}^{(2)}
.

Now we show the existence of the critical point
\theta_{>}
for
E_{1}^{(2)}
(the proof of
\theta_{<}
for
\mathcal{E}_{1}^{(2)}
is analogous). Let
\theta_{-}:=\theta_{k,-}-\delta_{0}^{10}
and
\theta_{+}:=\theta_{k,-}+\delta_{0}^{10}
. Recalling Lemma
3.13
and (
3.21
), we have

E_{1}^{(2)}(\theta_{+})\geq E_{0,+}(\theta_{+})-O(\varepsilon)\geq E_{0,+}(\theta_{k,-})+\delta_{0}^{11}-O(\varepsilon)>E_{1}^{(2)}(\theta_{k,-})

and

E_{1}^{(2)}(\theta_{-})\geq E_{0,-}(\theta_{-})-O(\varepsilon)\geq E_{0,-}(\theta_{k,-})+\delta_{0}^{11}-O(\varepsilon)>E_{1}^{(2)}(\theta_{k,-}).

Thus
E_{1}^{(2)}
must have a critical point
\theta_{>}\in[\theta_{-},\theta_{+}]
by mean value theorem.
The two-monotonicity interval structure follows from item
(b)
of Proposition
3.18
and Lemma
2.3
. Thus we finish the proof of
(a)
.

If
E_{1}^{(2)}(\theta_{s})=\mathcal{E}_{1}^{(2)}(\theta_{s})
, by item
(c)
of Proposition
3.18
,
E_{1}^{(2)}
is strictly increasing and
\mathcal{E}_{1}^{(2)}
is strictly decreasing. Thus
E_{1}^{(2)}>\mathcal{E}_{1}^{(2)}
on the right of
\theta_{s}
and
E_{1}^{(2)}<\mathcal{E}_{1}^{(2)}
on the left of
\theta_{s}
. From the above observation, the proof of
(b)
is obvious.
∎

Remark 3.7
.

While in the previous proposition, we resolve a pair of double resonant Rellich functions as two piecewise
C^{1}
Rellich curves satisfying a Morse condition, we still crucially utilize the
C^{2}
-smoothness of the potential
v
to ensure the
C^{2}
smoothness of Rellich functions in order to apply Feynman-Hellman formulas (c.f.
(2)
of Lemma
2.4
). It will be interesting that the
C^{2}
-cosine type assumption on
v
could be weakened to the piecewise
C^{1}
one with two critical points satisfying some Morse condition.

3.2.3.
Domain adjustment

In the above two parts, for every phase domain
I_{i}^{(1)}
or
I_{k,\cup}^{(2)}
, we have found a block
B_{1}^{(1)}
or
B_{1}^{(2)}
such that any resonant Rellich child is Morse with two-monotonicity interval structure (c.f. Proposition
3.10
and
3.20
). To finish the construction of the first generation Rellich functions
\mathcal{C}_{1}
, we must modify the domain so that the Rellich child has the same image on each of its monotone interval to prepare for the construction of the next scale.
This technical argument was also first introduced in
[
FV21
]
.

Proposition 3.21
.

We have the following:

(a).

In the simple resonance case, for the Rellich function
E_{1}^{(1)}
defined on
I^{(1)}_{i}
, we can find
I(E_{1}^{(1)})\subset I^{(1)}_{i}
with

|I^{(1)}_{i}\setminus I(E_{1}^{(1)})|\leq\delta_{0}^{5},

(3.23)

such that
E_{1}^{(1)}
maintains the two-monotonicity interval structure on
I(E_{1}^{(1)})
and has the same image
J(E_{1}^{(1)}):=E_{1}^{(1)}(I(E_{1}^{(1)}))
on each monotone component of
I(E_{1}^{(1)})
.

(b)
.

In the double resonance case, for the Rellich functions
E_{1,>}^{(2)},E_{1,<}^{(2)}
defined on
I^{(2)}_{k,\cup}
, we can find
I(E_{1,>}^{(2)})\subset[\operatorname{inf}I^{(2)}_{k,-},\operatorname{sup}I^{(2)}_{k,+}-k\cdot\omega],I(E_{1,<}^{(2)})\subset[\operatorname{inf}I^{(2)}_{k,+}-k\cdot\omega,\operatorname{sup}I^{(2)}_{k,-}]
with

[\theta_{k,-}-\delta_{0},\theta_{k,-}+\delta_{0}]\subset I(E_{1,>}^{(2)})\cap I(E_{1,<}^{(2)}),

(3.24)

|[\operatorname{inf}I^{(2)}_{k,-},\operatorname{sup}I^{(2)}_{k,+}-k\cdot\omega]\setminus I(E_{1,>}^{(2)})|\leq\delta_{0}^{5},

|[\operatorname{inf}I^{(2)}_{k,+}-k\cdot\omega,\operatorname{sup}I^{(2)}_{k,-}]\setminus I(E_{1,<}^{(2)})|\leq\delta_{0}^{5},

such that the Rellich function
E_{1,>}^{(2)}
(resp.
E_{1,<}^{(2)}
) maintains the two-monotonicity interval structure on
I(E_{1,>}^{(2)})
(resp.
I(E_{1,<}^{(2)})
) and has the same image
J(E_{1,>}^{(2)}):=E_{1,>}^{(2)}(I(E_{1,>}^{(2)}))
(resp.
J(E_{1,<}^{(2)}):=E_{1,<}^{(2)}(I(E_{1,<}^{(2)}))
) on each monotone component of
I(E_{1,>}^{(2)})
(resp.
I(E_{1,<}^{(2)})
).

We abuse the notation and henceforth denote by
E_{1}^{(1)},E_{1,>}^{(2)},E_{1,<}^{(2)}
the restrictions of the Rellich functions to their modified domains
I(E_{1}^{(1)}),I(E_{1,>}^{(2)}),I(E_{1,<}^{(2)})
.

Proof.

For the simple resonance case, recalling the two-monotonicity interval structure (c.f. Proposition
3.10
), we denote by
(E_{1}^{(1)})_{+}
(resp.
(E_{1}^{(1)})_{-}
) the restriction of
E_{1}^{(1)}
to the increasing (resp. decreasing) interval on
I_{i}^{(1)}
and define

\displaystyle J(E_{1}^{(1)})

\displaystyle:=\operatorname{Image}(E_{1}^{(1)})_{+}\cap\operatorname{Image}(E_{1}^{(1)})_{-},

\displaystyle I(E_{1}^{(1)})_{+}

\displaystyle:=((E_{1}^{(1)})_{+})^{-1}(J(E_{1}^{(1)})),\ I(E_{1}^{(1)})_{-}:=((E_{1}^{(1)})_{-})^{-1}(J(E_{1}^{(1)})),

\displaystyle I(E_{1}^{(1)})

\displaystyle:=I(E_{1}^{(1)})_{+}\cup I(E_{1}^{(1)})_{-}.

We only need to verify (
3.23
). By (
3.9
), we deduce

|J_{i}^{(1)}\Delta\operatorname{Image}(E_{1}^{(1)})_{+}|,\ |J_{i}^{(1)}\Delta\operatorname{Image}(E_{1}^{(1)})_{-}|\lesssim\varepsilon,

where
X\Delta Y:=(X\setminus Y)\cup(Y\setminus X)
for sets
X,Y
.
Thus

|\operatorname{Image}(E_{1}^{(1)})_{+}\Delta\operatorname{Image}(E_{1}^{(1)})_{-}|\lesssim\varepsilon,

from which we obtain

|\operatorname{Image}(E_{1}^{(1)})_{+}\setminus J(E_{1}^{(1)})|,\ |\operatorname{Image}(E_{1}^{(1)})_{-}\setminus J(E_{1}^{(1)})|\lesssim\varepsilon.

Thus by the Morse condition of
(E_{1}^{(1)})_{\pm}
(c.f. Proposition
3.8
) and Lemma
2.3
, we get upper bound for the contraction of the preimage

\displaystyle|I^{(1)}_{i}\setminus I(E_{1}^{(1)})|

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle|((E_{1}^{(1)})_{+})^{-1}(\operatorname{Image}(E_{1}^{(1)})_{+}\setminus J(E_{1}^{(1)}))|+|((E_{1}^{(1)})_{-})^{-1}(\operatorname{Image}(E_{1}^{(1)})_{-}\setminus J(E_{1}^{(1)}))|

\displaystyle\lesssim

\displaystyle\sqrt{\varepsilon}<\delta_{0}^{5}.

Thus we finish the proof of
(a)
.

The proof of
(b)
is analogous. Recalling the two-monotonicity interval structure (c.f. Proposition
3.40
), we denote by
(E_{1,\bullet}^{(2)})_{+}
(resp.
(E_{1,\bullet}^{(2)})_{-}
) the restriction of
E_{1,\bullet}^{(2)}
to the increasing (resp. decreasing) interval on
I_{k,\cup}^{(2)}
and define

\displaystyle J(E_{1,\bullet}^{(2)})

\displaystyle:=\operatorname{Image}(E_{1,\bullet}^{(2)})_{+}\cap\operatorname{Image}(E_{1,\bullet}^{(2)})_{-},

\displaystyle I(E_{1,\bullet}^{(2)})_{+}

\displaystyle:=((E_{1,\bullet}^{(2)})_{+})^{-1}(J(E_{1,\bullet}^{(2)})),\ I(E_{1,\bullet}^{(2)})_{-}:=((E_{1,\bullet}^{(2)})_{-})^{-1}(J(E_{1,\bullet}^{(2)})),

\displaystyle I(E_{1,\bullet}^{(2)})

\displaystyle:=I(E_{1,\bullet}^{(2)})_{+}\cup I(E_{1,\bullet}^{(2)})_{-},

where
\bullet\in\{>,<\}.

As the above argument, we can employ approximation (
3.21
) and the Morse condition of
E_{1,>}^{(2)},E_{1,<}^{(2)}
to prove

|[\operatorname{inf}I^{(2)}_{k,-},\operatorname{sup}I^{(2)}_{k,+}-k\cdot\omega]\setminus I(E_{1,>}^{(2)})|\lesssim\sqrt{\varepsilon}<\delta_{0}^{5},

|[\operatorname{inf}I^{(2)}_{k,+}-k\cdot\omega,\operatorname{sup}I^{(2)}_{k,-}]\setminus I(E_{1,<}^{(2)})|\lesssim\sqrt{\varepsilon}<\delta_{0}^{5}.

Finally, (
3.24
) follows from the uniform bound
D
of the derivative of the Rellich functions and
|J^{(2)}_{k}|\sim\delta_{0}^{\frac{1}{100}}\gg\delta_{0}.

∎

I(E_{1}^{(1)})_{-}
I(E_{1}^{(1)})_{+}
J(E_{1}^{(1)})
(E_{1}^{(1)})_{-}
(E_{1}^{(1)})_{+}
I_{i,-}^{(1)}
I_{i,+}^{(1)}
contraction
\leq\delta_{0}^{5}

Figure 4.
A carton illustration of the domain adjustment of the simple resonant case with no critical point: After a domain contraction smaller than
\delta_{0}^{5}
, the Rellich child
E_{1}^{(1)}
has the same image on each of its monotone interval.

3.2.4.
Green’s function estimates

In this part, we relate the first scale resonance to the Rellich function collection
\mathcal{C}_{1}
we have constructed and establish the Green’s function estimates for
1
-nonresonant sets with
1
-regularity condition.

In order to handle technicalities that arise near the boundaries of the Rellich functions, we introduce the following definition for “modified codomains”. With the notation from Proposition
3.21
, for
E_{1}^{(1)}\in\mathcal{C}_{1}^{(1)}
, we define

\displaystyle\tilde{J}(E_{1}^{(1)}):=\left\{\begin{aligned} &[\inf J(E_{1}^{(1)})+\frac{9}{8}\delta_{0},\sup J(E_{1}^{(1)})-\frac{9}{8}\delta_{0}]\text{ \ if $E_{1}^{(1)}$ has no critical point in $I(E_{1}^{(1)})$,}\\
&(-\infty,\sup J(E_{1}^{(1)})-\frac{9}{8}\delta_{0}]\text{ \ if $E_{1}^{(1)}$ attains minimum at a critical point in $I(E_{1}^{(1)})$,}\\
&[\inf J(E_{1}^{(1)})+\frac{9}{8}\delta_{0},+\infty)\text{ \ if $E_{1}^{(1)}$ attains maximum at a critical point in $I(E_{1}^{(1)})$,}\\
\end{aligned}\right.

and for
E_{1,\bullet}^{(2)}\in\mathcal{C}_{1}^{(2)}(\bullet\in\{>,<\})
, we define

\tilde{J}(E_{1,\bullet}^{(2)}):=[\inf J(E_{1,<}^{(2)})+\frac{9}{8}\delta_{0},\sup J(E_{1,>}^{(2)})-\frac{9}{8}\delta_{0}].

Fixing
\theta^{*},E^{*}
, let
E_{1}^{(j)}\in\mathcal{C}_{1}
be the Rellich function such that
E^{*}\in\tilde{J}(E_{1}^{(j)})\ (j\in\{1,2\}).

Define the set of
1
-resonant points (relative to
(\theta^{*},E^{*})
and
E_{1}^{(j)}
) as:

(1)
.
j=1
,

S_{1}(\theta^{*},E^{*}):=\{x\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}:\ \theta^{*}+x\cdot\omega\in I(E_{1}^{(1)}),\ |E_{1}^{(1)}(\theta^{*}+x\cdot\omega)-E^{*}|<\delta_{1}^{(1)}\},

(2)
.
j=2
,

S_{1}(\theta^{*},E^{*}):=\{x\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}:\ \theta^{*}+x\cdot\omega\in I(E_{1,>}^{(2)})\cup I(E_{1,<}^{(2)}),\ \min_{\bullet\in\{>,<\}}|E_{1,\bullet}^{(2)}(\theta^{*}+x\cdot\omega)-E^{*}|<\delta_{1}^{(2)}\},

where
\delta_{1}^{(j)}:=e^{-(l_{1}^{(j)})^{\frac{2}{3}}}
.

We say that a set
\Lambda\subset\mathbb{Z}^{d}
is
1
-nonresonant if
\Lambda\cap S_{1}(\theta^{*},E^{*})=\emptyset
. Moreover, we say that a set
\Lambda\subset\mathbb{Z}^{d}
is
1
-regular if
(\Lambda_{2l_{1}^{(j)}}+x)\subset\Lambda
for any
x\in S_{0}(\theta^{*},E^{*})\cap\Lambda.
Finally, we say that a set is
1
-good if it is both
1
-nonresonant and
1
-regular. With the above definitions, we can now prove

Theorem 3.22
.

Fix
\theta^{*},E^{*},E_{1}^{(j)}\in\mathcal{C}_{1}
such that
E^{*}\in\tilde{J}(E_{1}^{(j)})
and a finite set
\Lambda\subset\mathbb{Z}^{d}
. If
\Lambda
is
1
-good, then for
|\theta-\theta^{*}|<\delta_{1}^{(j)}/(10D),|E-E^{*}|<\delta_{1}^{(j)}/5
,

\displaystyle\|G_{\Lambda}(\theta,E)\|

\displaystyle\leq 10(\delta_{1}^{(j)})^{-1},

\displaystyle\ |G_{\Lambda}(\theta,E;x,y)|

\displaystyle\leq e^{-\gamma_{1}\|x-y\|_{1}},\ \|x-y\|_{1}\geq(l_{1}^{(j)})^{\frac{5}{6}},

where
\gamma_{1}=(1-O((l_{1}^{(j)})^{-\frac{1}{30}}))\gamma_{0}
.

Proof.

Recalling the set
S_{0}(\theta^{*},E^{*})
(c.f. (
3.1
)), we define
S_{0}^{\Lambda}:=S_{0}(\theta^{*},E^{*})\cap\Lambda
. If
S_{0}^{\Lambda}=\emptyset
, then
\Lambda
is
0
-nonresonant and the theorem follows from Theorem
3.1
. We thus assume
S_{0}^{\Lambda}
is nonempty. In order to apply resolvent identity, we wish to find a finite family
\{B_{1}^{(j)}+p\}_{p\in\bar{S}_{0}^{\Lambda}}
of translations of
B_{1}^{(j)}
such that each
x\in S_{0}^{\Lambda}
is near the center of
B_{1}^{(j)}+p
for some
p\in\bar{S}_{0}^{\Lambda}
and
\|G_{B_{1}^{(j)}+p}(\theta^{*},E^{*})\|\leq(\delta_{1}^{(j)})^{-1}

for all
p\in\bar{S}_{0}^{\Lambda}
. We do so by case analysis:

(1).

j=1
: Assume that
E^{*}\in\tilde{J}(E_{1}^{(1)})
. We first prove that for any
x\in S_{0}^{\Lambda}
,
\theta^{*}+x\cdot\omega\in I(E_{1}^{(1)})
. The proof proceeds also by case analysis:

(a)
.

E_{1}^{(1)}
has no critical point in
I(E_{1}^{(1)})
: In this case,
I(E_{1}^{(1)})
is a union of two disjoint intervals. Since
E^{*}\in\tilde{J}(E_{1}^{(1)})
and
x\in S_{0}^{\Lambda}
, by the definition of
\tilde{J}(E_{1}^{(1)})
, we obtain

v(\theta^{*}+x\cdot\omega)\in[\inf J(E_{1}^{(1)})+\frac{1}{8}\delta_{0},\sup J(E_{1}^{(1)})-\frac{1}{8}\delta_{0}].

Since
j=1
, by approximation (
3.9
), we have

[\inf J(E_{1}^{(1)})+\frac{1}{8}\delta_{0},\sup J(E_{1}^{(1)})-\frac{1}{8}\delta_{0}]\subset v_{\pm}(I(E_{1}^{(1)})_{\pm}).

Thus
v(\theta^{*}+x\cdot\omega)\in v_{\pm}(I(E_{1}^{(1)})_{\pm})
and so
\theta^{*}+x\cdot\omega\in I(E_{1}^{(1)})
.

(b)
.

E_{1}^{(1)}
has a critical point in
I(E_{1}^{(1)})
: In this case,
I(E_{1}^{(1)})
is a single interval. Suppose that this critical point is a minimum (the other case is analogous), then
v
likewise achieves its minimum at a critical point in
I(E_{1}^{(1)})
. As above, we have

v(\theta^{*}+x\cdot\omega)\in[\inf J^{(1)}_{i},\sup J(E_{1}^{(1)})-\frac{1}{8}\delta_{0}].

and

[\inf J^{(1)}_{i},\sup J(E_{1}^{(1)})-\frac{1}{8}\delta_{0}]\subset v_{\pm}(I(E_{1}^{(1)})_{\pm}).

Thus
\theta^{*}+x\cdot\omega\in I(E_{1}^{(1)})
.

For
j=1
, we define
\bar{S}_{0}^{\Lambda}=S_{0}^{\Lambda}
.

Since
|v(\theta^{*}+x\cdot\omega)-E^{*}|\leq\delta_{0}
and
\Lambda
is
1
-nonresonant, we have

\delta_{1}^{(1)}\leq|E_{1}^{(1)}(\theta^{*}+x\cdot\omega)-E^{*}|\leq\frac{9}{8}\delta_{0}.

By the eigenvalue separation estimates from Proposition
3.6
, it follows that
|\hat{E}-E^{*}|\geq\delta_{1}^{(1)}
for any eigenvalue
\hat{E}
of
H_{B_{1}^{(1)}+x}(\theta^{*})
, and so

\|G_{B_{1}^{(1)}+x}(\theta^{*},E^{*})\|\leq(\delta_{1}^{(1)})^{-1}.

(2).

j=2
: Assume that
E^{*}\in\tilde{J}(E_{1}^{(2)})
and
E_{1,\bullet}^{(2)}

(\bullet\in\{>,<\})
are the Rellich children generated from the double resonance interval
J_{k}^{(2)}
. We first prove that for any
x\in S_{0}^{\Lambda}
,
\theta^{*}+p\cdot\omega\in I(E_{1,>}^{(2)})\cup I(E_{1,<}^{(2)})
for some
p\in\{x,x-k\}
.

Since
E^{*}\in\tilde{J}(E_{1}^{(2)})
and
x\in S_{0}^{\Lambda}
, by the definition of
\tilde{J}(E_{1}^{(2)})
, we obtain

v(\theta^{*}+x\cdot\omega)\in[\inf J(E_{1,<}^{(2)})+\frac{1}{8}\delta_{0},\sup J(E_{1,>}^{(2)})-\frac{1}{8}\delta_{0}].

Since
j=2
, by approximation (
3.21
), we have

[\inf J(E_{1,<}^{(2)})+\frac{1}{8}\delta_{0},\sup J(E_{1,>}^{(2)})-\frac{1}{8}\delta_{0}]\subset v_{-}\left([\inf I(E_{1,>}^{(2)}),\sup I(E_{1,<}^{(2)})]\right)

and

[\inf J(E_{1,<}^{(2)})+\frac{1}{8}\delta_{0},\sup J(E_{1,>}^{(2)})-\frac{1}{8}\delta_{0}]\subset v_{+}\left([\inf I(E_{1,<}^{(2)}),\sup I(E_{1,>}^{(2)})]+k\cdot\omega\right).

Thus
\theta^{*}+x\cdot\omega\in[\inf I(E_{1,>}^{(2)}),\sup I(E_{1,<}^{(2)})]\cup[\inf I(E_{1,<}^{(2)}),\sup I(E_{1,>}^{(2)})]+k\cdot\omega
. It follows that
\theta^{*}+x\cdot\omega\in[\inf I(E_{1,>}^{(2)}),\sup I(E_{1,<}^{(2)})]
or
\theta^{*}+(x-k)\cdot\omega\in[\inf I(E_{1,<}^{(2)}),\sup I(E_{1,>}^{(2)})]
. Thus
\theta^{*}+p\cdot\omega\in I(E_{1,>}^{(2)})\cup I(E_{1,<}^{(2)})
for some
p\in\{x,x-k\}
.

For
j=2
, we define
\bar{S}_{0}^{\Lambda}
to be the set of all point
p
coming from
x\in S_{0}^{\Lambda}
as above.
Since
\Lambda
is
1
-regular and
\|k\|_{1}\leq 10l_{1}^{(1)}<2l_{1}^{(2)}
, it follows that
\bar{S}_{0}^{\Lambda}\subset\Lambda
and since
\Lambda
is
1
-nonresonant, by the eigenvalue separation estimates from Proposition
3.12
, it follows that
|\hat{E}-E^{*}|\geq\delta_{1}^{(2)}
for any eigenvalue
\hat{E}
of
H_{B_{1}^{(2)}+p}(\theta^{*})
, and so

\|G_{B_{1}^{(2)}+p}(\theta^{*},E^{*})\|\leq(\delta_{1}^{(2)})^{-1}.

For both
j=1,2
, by the
1
-regularity of
\Lambda
,
(B_{1}^{(j)}+p)\subset\Lambda
for all
p\in\bar{S}_{0}^{\Lambda}
. Moreover, by Diophantine condition, the separation of
p\in\bar{S}_{0}^{\Lambda}
is larger than the size of
B_{1}^{(j)}
, thus the blocks
\{B_{1}^{(j)}+p\}_{p\in\bar{S}_{0}^{\Lambda}}
are non-overlapping. The Green’s function estimates follow from a standard application of resolvent identity as we will show below. Since
j
has little effect in the proof, we omit the superscript “
(j)
”.

First, we prove the case that
\Lambda=B_{1}+p
is a single
1
-nonresonant block. By the previous discussion, we have

\|G_{\Lambda}(\theta^{*},E^{*})\|\leq\delta_{1}^{-1}.

It follows from Neumann series argument that for
|\theta-\theta^{*}|<\delta_{1}/(10D)
and
|E-E^{*}|<\frac{2}{5}\delta_{1}
,

\|G_{\Lambda}(\theta,E)\|\leq 2\delta_{1}^{-1}.

Let
x,y\in\Lambda
satisfy
\|x-y\|_{1}\geq l_{1}^{\frac{4}{5}}
. Since
G_{\Lambda}
is self-adjoint, we may assume
\|x-p\|_{1}\geq l_{1}^{\frac{3}{4}}
. Let
\Omega_{1,p}
be an
l_{1}^{\frac{2}{3}}
-size cube centered at
p
. Then
\Lambda\setminus\Omega_{1,p}
is
0
-nonresonant since
S_{0}^{\Lambda}\subset\Omega_{1,p}
. We omit the dependence on
E,\theta
of Green’s functions. By the resolvent identity, we obtain

\displaystyle|G_{\Lambda}(\theta,E;x,y)|

\displaystyle=|G_{\Lambda\setminus\Omega_{1,p}}(x,y)\chi(y)+\sum_{z,z^{\prime}}G_{\Lambda\setminus\Omega_{1,p}}(x,z)\Gamma_{z,z^{\prime}}G_{\Lambda}(z^{\prime},y)|

\displaystyle\leq e^{-\gamma_{0}\|x-y\|_{1}}+C(d)\sup_{z,z^{\prime}}e^{-\gamma_{0}\|x-z\|_{1}}|G_{\Lambda}(z^{\prime},y)|

\displaystyle\leq e^{-\gamma_{0}\|x-y\|_{1}}+C(d)\sup_{z,z^{\prime}}e^{-\gamma_{0}\|x-z\|_{1}}e^{-\gamma_{0}(\|z^{\prime}-y\|_{1}-l_{1}^{\frac{3}{4}})}\delta_{1}^{-1}

\displaystyle\leq e^{-\gamma^{\prime}_{0}\|x-y\|_{1}}

with
\gamma^{\prime}_{0}=(1-O(l_{1}^{-\frac{1}{30}}))\gamma_{0}
, where we used if
\|z^{\prime}-y\|\leq l_{1}^{\frac{3}{4}}
, then

|G_{\Lambda}(z^{\prime},y)|\leq\|G_{\Lambda}\|\leq 2\delta_{1}^{-1}\leq 2e^{-\gamma_{0}(\|z^{\prime}-y\|_{1}-l_{1}^{\frac{3}{4}})}\delta_{1}^{-1},

if
\|z^{\prime}-y\|\geq l_{1}^{\frac{3}{4}}
, then

\displaystyle|G_{\Lambda}(z^{\prime},y)|=|G_{\Lambda}(y,z^{\prime})|

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{w,w^{\prime}}|G_{\Lambda\setminus\Omega_{1,p}}(y,w)\Gamma_{w,w^{\prime}}G_{\Lambda}(w^{\prime},z^{\prime})|

\displaystyle\leq C(d)e^{-\gamma_{0}\|y-w\|_{1}}\|G_{\Lambda}\|

\displaystyle\leq C(d)e^{-\gamma_{0}(\|y-z^{\prime}\|_{1}-l_{1}^{\frac{3}{4}})}\delta_{1}^{-1}

and
\delta_{1}^{-1}=e^{l_{1}^{\frac{2}{3}}}\ll e^{\gamma_{0}\|x-y\|_{1}}
to bound the second term. Thus we finish the case that
\Lambda
is a single
1
-nonresonant block.

Now assume
\Lambda
is an arbitrary
1
-good set. We must show that
G_{\Lambda}
does exist. By Schur’s test, it suffices to prove

\sup_{x}\sum_{y}|G_{\Lambda}(\theta,E+io;x,y)|<C<\infty.

(3.25)

Define

\Lambda^{\prime}:=\Lambda\setminus\bigcup_{p\in\bar{S}_{0}^{\Lambda}}\Omega_{1,p}.

Then
\Lambda^{\prime}
is
0
-nonresonant since
S_{0}^{\Lambda}\subset\cup_{p\in\bar{S}_{0}^{\Lambda}}\Omega_{1,p}.
Let
\tilde{\Omega}_{1,p}
be a
2l_{1}^{\frac{2}{3}}
-size cube centered at
p
. For
x\in\Lambda\setminus\cup_{p\in\bar{S}_{0}^{\Lambda}}\tilde{\Omega}_{1,p}
, by resolvent identity, we have

\displaystyle\sum_{y}|G_{\Lambda}(x,y)|

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{y}|G_{\Lambda^{\prime}}(x,y)|+\sum_{z,z^{\prime},y}|G_{\Lambda^{\prime}}(x,z)\Gamma_{z,z^{\prime}}G_{\Lambda}(z^{\prime},y)|

\displaystyle\leq C(d)\delta_{0}^{-1}+C(d)e^{-l_{1}^{\frac{2}{3}}}\sup_{z^{\prime}}\sum_{y}|G_{\Lambda}(z^{\prime},y)|.

For
x\in\tilde{\Omega}_{1,p}
, by resolvent identity, we have

\displaystyle\sum_{y}|G_{\Lambda}(x,y)|

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{y}|G_{B_{1}+p}(x,y)|+\sum_{z,z^{\prime},y}|G_{B_{1}+p}(x,z)\Gamma_{z,z^{\prime}}G_{\Lambda}(z^{\prime},y)|

\displaystyle\leq\delta_{1}^{-2}+C(d)e^{-\frac{1}{2}l_{1}}\sup_{z^{\prime}}\sum_{y}|G_{\Lambda}(z^{\prime},y)|.

Taking supremum for
x
on the left hand side of the above two inequalities, we get

\sup_{x}\sum_{y}|G_{\Lambda}(x,y)|\leq\delta_{1}^{-2}+\frac{1}{2}\sup_{x}\sum_{y}|G_{\Lambda}(x,y)|,

thus

\sup_{x}\sum_{y}|G_{\Lambda}(x,y)|\leq 2\delta_{1}^{-2},

which gives (
3.25
).
Note that for
|\theta-\theta^{*}|<\delta_{1}/(10D)
and
|E-E^{*}|<\frac{2}{5}\delta_{1}
,
G_{\Lambda}(\theta,E)
does exist. Then we obtain
\operatorname{dist}(\sigma(H_{\Lambda}(\theta)),E^{*})\geq\frac{2}{5}\delta_{1}
and
\operatorname{dist}(\sigma(H_{\Lambda}(\theta)),E)\geq\frac{1}{5}\delta_{1}
for all
|E-E^{*}|<\frac{1}{5}\delta_{1}
. Thus we get the desired operator norm

\|G_{\Lambda}(\theta,E)\|=\|\left(H_{\Lambda}(\theta)-E\right)^{-1}\|=\frac{1}{\operatorname{dist}(\sigma(H_{\Lambda}(\theta)),E)}\leq 10\delta_{1}^{-1}.

It remains to prove the off-diagonal decay of
G_{\Lambda}
.
Let
x,y\in\Lambda
be such that
\|x-y\|_{1}\geq l_{1}^{\frac{5}{6}}
. We define

B_{x}:=\left\{\begin{aligned} &\Lambda_{\lambda_{1}^{\frac{1}{2}}}(x)\cap\Lambda\quad\text{if }x\in\Lambda\setminus\bigcup_{p\in\bar{S}_{0}^{\Lambda}}\tilde{\Omega}_{1,p},\text{ \ (Choice A)}\\
&B_{1}+p\ \quad\quad\ \text{if }x\in\tilde{\Omega}_{1,p}.\text{\ (Choice B)}\ \end{aligned}\right.

Then
B_{x}
has the following two properties:
(1).

B_{x}
is either a
0
-nonresonant set (Choice A) or a
1
-nonresonant block (Choice B);
(2).

x
is far away from the relative boundary
\partial_{\Lambda}B_{x}
. We can iterate the resolvent identity to obtain

\displaystyle|G_{\Lambda}(x,y)|

\displaystyle\leq\prod_{s=0}^{L-1}(C(d)l_{1}^{d}e^{-\gamma_{0}^{\prime}\|x_{s}-x_{s+1}\|_{1}})|G_{\Lambda}(x_{L},y)|

\displaystyle\leq e^{-\gamma_{0}^{\prime\prime}\|x-x_{L}\|_{1}}|G_{\Lambda}(x_{L},y)|,

(3.26)

where
x_{0}:=x
and
x_{s+1}\in\partial B_{x_{s}}
with
\|x_{s+1}-x_{s}\|_{1}\geq l_{1}^{\frac{1}{2}}
for Choice A and
\|x_{s+1}-x_{s}\|_{1}\geq\frac{1}{2}l_{1}\gg l_{1}^{\frac{5}{6}}
for Choice B. We stop the iteration until
y\in B_{x_{L}}
.
By resolvent identity, we get

\displaystyle|G_{\Lambda}(x_{L},y)|

\displaystyle\leq|G_{B_{x_{L}}}(x_{L},y)|+\sum_{z,z^{\prime}}|G_{B_{x_{L}}}(x_{L},z)\Gamma_{z,z^{\prime}}G_{\Lambda}(z^{\prime},y)|

\displaystyle\leq C(d)e^{-\gamma_{0}^{\prime}(\|x_{L}-y\|_{1}-l_{1}^{\frac{4}{5}})}\delta_{1}^{-1},

(3.27)

where we have used the off-diagonal exponential decay of
G_{B_{x_{L}}}
and the operator norm estimate
\|G_{\Lambda}\|\leq 10\delta_{1}^{-1}
. Since
\|x-y\|_{1}\geq l_{1}^{\frac{5}{6}}
, (
3.26
) together with (
3.27
) gives the desired off-diagonal estimate

|G_{\Lambda}(x,y)|\leq e^{-\gamma_{1}\|x-y\|_{1}}

with
\gamma_{1}=(1-O(l_{1}^{-\frac{1}{30}}))\gamma_{0}
. Thus we finish the proof.
∎

3.3.
Induction definitions and propositions

In this part, we will list the most important properties of the Rellich functions
\mathcal{C}_{n}
in our induction hypothesis. The induction will proceed as follows: we suppose that we have inductively
constructed Rellich functions
E_{n}:I(E_{n})\rightarrow J(E_{n})
satisfying Morse condition and two-monotonicity interval structure and established Green’s function estimates on
n
-good sets (relative to
E_{n}
) for energies
E^{*}\in\tilde{J}(E_{n})
, where
\tilde{J}(E_{n})
is an energy interval near
the codomain of
E_{n}
. Under these assumptions, we first classify energy regions
J^{(j)}_{i}\subset J(E_{n})
as being simple or double resonant (indicated by
j\in\{1,2\}
) and prevent recurrence to those energy regions for long times. For each energy region, the resonant sites for
E_{n}
will be so well-separated that we can find a block
B_{n+1}^{(j)}
of
l_{n+1}^{(j)}
-size having a large annulus with Green’s function estimates by our induction assumptions. Then we employ the eigenvalue perturbation theory to construct the Rellich function
E_{n+1}^{(j)}
of
H_{B_{n+1}^{(j)}}
maintaining the induction structure near
E_{n}
. Finally, we prove Green’s function estimates on
(n+1)
-good sets.

We say that
E_{n+1}^{(j)}
is a child of
E_{n}
and
E_{n}
is the parent of
E_{n+1}^{(j)}
, denoted by
E_{n+1}^{(j)}\in\mathcal{C}^{(j)}(E_{n})
.
We introduce some notation to refer to different parts of our Rellich tree. Recall that
\mathcal{C}(E_{n})
denotes the collection of all immediate children of the Rellich curve
E_{n}
, and that
\mathcal{C}_{n}
denotes the collection of all
n
-scale Rellich functions, i.e., the
n^{\text{th }}
“generation” of the tree. For a Rellich function
E_{m}\in\mathcal{C}_{m},m\leqslant n
, we denote by
\mathcal{C}_{n}(E_{m})
the collection of all the
n
-scale descendants of
E_{m}
; i.e., for a
m
-scale Rellich function
E_{m}
,
\mathcal{C}_{m}(E_{m})=\left\{E_{m}\right\}
, and for all
s\geqslant m
, if
E_{s}\in\mathcal{C}_{s}\left(E_{m}\right)
and
E_{s+1}\in\mathcal{C}\left(E_{s}\right)
, then
E_{s+1}\in\mathcal{C}_{s+1}\left(E_{m}\right)
. For
0\leq m\leq n
, we say that
E_{n}
is a descendant of
E_{m}
and
E_{m}
is an ancestor of
E_{n}
if
E_{n}\in\mathcal{C}_{n}(E_{m})
.

Now we define the induction parameters:

l_{n}^{(j)}:=(|\log\varepsilon_{0}|^{4^{n}})^{j},\ n\geq 1,\ j\in\{1,2\}\quad\text{ (the $n$-scale length)}

and

\delta_{n}^{(j)}:=e^{-(l_{n}^{(j)})^{\frac{2}{3}}},\ n\geq 1,\ j\in\{1,2\}\quad\text{ (the $n$-scale resonance)},

where the superscript “
(j)
” may be omitted if not emphasized.

Subject to these definitions, we suppose that the following induction proposition holds.

Proposition 3.23

(Induction hypotheses of scale
n
)
.

Hypothesis 1
.

For
1\leq m\leq n
, we have collections

\displaystyle\mathcal{C}_{m}^{(j)}

\displaystyle=\bigcup_{E_{m-1}\in\mathcal{C}_{m-1}}\mathcal{C}^{(j)}(E_{m-1}),\ \ j\in\{1,2\},

\displaystyle\mathcal{C}_{m}

\displaystyle=\mathcal{C}_{m}^{(1)}\cup\mathcal{C}_{m}^{(2)}

of Rellich functions

E_{m}^{(j)}:I(E_{m}^{(j)})\rightarrow J(E_{m}^{(j)})

with
\delta_{m-2}^{10}\lesssim|I(E_{m}^{(j)})|\lesssim\delta_{m-2}^{5},|J(E_{m}^{(j)})|\sim\delta_{m-2}^{10}
for
m\geq 2
and
\delta_{0}^{\frac{1}{100}}\lesssim|I(E_{1}^{(j)})|\lesssim\delta_{0}^{\frac{1}{200}},|J(E_{1}^{(j)})|\sim\delta_{0}^{\frac{1}{100}}
for
m=1
of
H_{B_{m}^{(j)}}
, where
B_{m}^{(j)}
is a
l_{m}^{(j)}
-size block centered at origin.

Hypothesis 2
.

Each
E_{m}\in\mathcal{C}_{m}
has a two-monotonicity interval structure with the same image on each monotonicity component, that is, there are two closed intervals
I(E_{m})_{\pm}
with disjoint interiors, such that

I(E_{m})=I(E_{m})_{+}\cup I(E_{m})_{-},

\pm E_{m}^{\prime}|_{I(E_{m})_{\pm}}\geq 0,

E_{m}(I(E_{m})_{+})=E_{m}(I(E_{m})_{-})=J(E_{m}).

Hypothesis 3
.

Each
E_{m}\in\mathcal{C}_{m}
satisfies the Morse condition:

If

|E_{m}^{\prime}(\theta)|\leq\delta_{m-2}^{2}\ (\delta_{-1}:=\delta_{0}^{\frac{1}{400}}),

then

|E_{m}^{\prime\prime}(\theta)|\geq 3-\sum_{s=0}^{n-1}\delta_{s}^{10}\geq 2

with a unique sign for all such
\theta
.

Hypothesis 4
.

The Rellich functions can be classified into the following three types:

Type 1
.

E_{m}
has no critical point and
I(E_{m})_{+}
and
I(E_{m})_{-}
are two disjoint intervals, and the derivative of
E_{m}
on each interval satisfies

\pm E_{m}^{\prime}|_{I(E_{m})_{\pm}}\gtrsim\delta_{m-2}^{10}.

I(E_{m})_{-}
I(E_{m})_{+}
J(E_{m})
(E_{m})_{-}
(E_{m})_{+}

Figure 5
.
A cartoon illustration of
E_{m}
belonging to
Type 1
.

Type 2
.

E_{m}
has a critical point and
I(E_{m})
is a single interval.

I(E_{m})_{-}
I(E_{m})_{+}
J(E_{m})
E_{m}

Figure 6
.
A cartoon illustration of
E_{m}
belonging to
Type 2
(the minimum case).

Type 3
.

E_{m}
has a Rellich brother and we denote the larger one by
E_{m,>}
and the smaller one by
E_{m,<}
. Both of their domains are single interval with a critical point (minimum for
E_{m,>}
and maximum for
E_{m,<}
) in their common domain
I(E_{m,>})\cap I(E_{m,<})\neq\emptyset
.

The two Rellich functions have resonance near their critical points:

0\leq\inf J(E_{m,>})-\sup J(E_{m,<})\leq 3\delta_{m},

and they are separated by two interlacing functions
\lambda_{m,\pm}
:

E_{m,<}(\theta)\leq\lambda_{m,\pm}(\theta)\leq E_{m,>}(\theta),

\pm\lambda_{m,\pm}^{\prime}(\theta)\geq\delta_{m-2}.

.
A cartoon illustration of of

: the graph on the left shows the eigenvalue separation case and the graph on the right shows the level crossing case.

Moreover, if
E_{m}(\theta)
belongs to
Type

1
or
2
, then
E_{m}(\theta)
is the unique eigenvalue of
H_{B_{m}^{(1)}}(\theta)
in the interval
[E_{m}(\theta)-3\delta_{m},E_{m}(\theta)+3\delta_{m}]
, and if
E_{m}(\theta)
belongs to
Type

3
, then
E_{m,<}(\theta)
and
E_{m,>}(\theta)
are the only two eigenvalues of
H_{B_{m}^{(2)}}(\theta)
in the interval
[\inf J(E_{m,<})-\frac{1}{2}\delta_{m-2},\sup J(E_{m,>})+\frac{1}{2}\delta_{m-2}]
. In all cases, the corresponding eigenfunction
\psi_{m}
decays exponentially:

|\psi_{m}(x)|\leq e^{-\frac{1}{4}\gamma_{0}\|x\|},\ \|x\|_{1}\geq l_{m}^{\frac{6}{7}}.

(3.28)

Hypothesis 5
.

We introduce the following definitions of “modified domain”
\tilde{J}(E_{m})
and
\tilde{\tilde{J}}(E_{m})
:

With the notation from
Hypothesis
4
, if
E_{m}
belongs to
Type

1
or
2
, we define

\displaystyle\tilde{J}(E_{m}):=\left\{\begin{aligned} &[\inf J(E_{m})+\frac{9}{8}\delta_{m-1},\sup J(E_{m})-\frac{9}{8}\delta_{m-1}]\text{ \ if $E_{m}$ has no critical point in $I(E_{m})$,}\\
&[\inf J(E_{m})-2\delta_{m},\sup J(E_{m})-\frac{9}{8}\delta_{m-1}]\text{ \ if $E_{m}$ has minimum at a critical point in $I(E_{m})$,}\\
&[\inf J(E_{m})+\frac{9}{8}\delta_{m-1},\sup J(E_{m})+2\delta_{m}]\text{ \ if $E_{m}$ has maximum at a critical point in $I(E_{m})$,}\\
\end{aligned}\right.

and if
E_{m}
belongs to
Type

3
, we define

\tilde{J}(E_{m}):=[\inf J(E_{m,<})+\frac{9}{8}\delta_{m-1},\sup J(E_{m,>})-\frac{9}{8}\delta_{m-1}].

\tilde{\tilde{J}}(E_{m})
is defined similarly, with
\frac{5}{4}
replacing
\frac{9}{8}
.

The codomain of
E_{m-1}
is almost covered by the codomains of its children in the following sense:

If
E_{m-1}
does not attain its maximum at a critical point, then

\sup\tilde{\tilde{J}}(E_{m-1})\leq\sup\bigcup_{E_{m}\in\mathcal{C}(E_{m-1})}\tilde{\tilde{J}}(E_{m}).

Similarly, if
E_{m-1}
does not attain its minimum at a critical point, then

\inf\tilde{\tilde{J}}\left(E_{m-1}\right)\geqslant\inf\bigcup_{E_{m}\in\mathcal{C}\left(E_{m-1}\right)}\tilde{\tilde{J}}(E_{m}).

If
E_{m}
does not attain its supremum
\sup J(E_{m})
at a critical point and
\sup J(E_{m})\neq

\sup_{E_{m}\in\mathcal{C}\left(E_{m-1}\right)}\sup J(E_{m})
, then there is some
\tilde{E}_{m}\in\mathcal{C}(E_{m-1})
such that

B_{\frac{11}{4}\delta_{m-1}}\left(\sup J(E_{m})\right)\subset J(\tilde{E}_{m}).

The same statement holds with inf in place of sup.

Hypothesis 6
.

It will be convenient to fix language describing “nonresonant” and “regular” sets at each scale. Fix
\theta^{*},E^{*}
. Let
E_{m}\in\mathcal{C}_{m}
be the Rellich function such that
E^{*}\in\tilde{J}(E_{m})
(if exists). The set of
m
-resonant points is defined as:

If
E_{m}
belongs
Type

1
or
2
, then

S_{m}(\theta^{*},E^{*}):=\{x\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}:\ \theta^{*}+x\cdot\omega\in I(E_{m}),\ |E_{m}(\theta^{*}+x\cdot\omega)-E^{*}|<\delta_{m}\},

and if
E_{m}
belongs to
Type

3
, then

S_{m}(\theta^{*},E^{*}):=\{x\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}:\ \theta^{*}+x\cdot\omega\in I(E_{m,<})\cup I(E_{m,>}),\ \min_{\bullet\in\{>,<\}}|E_{m,\bullet}(\theta^{*}+x\cdot\omega)-E^{*}|<\delta_{m}\}.

We say that a set
\Lambda\subset\mathbb{Z}^{d}
is
m
-nonresonant (relative to
(\theta^{*},E^{*})
and
E_{m}
) if
\Lambda\cap S_{m}(\theta^{*},E^{*})=\emptyset
and is
m
-regular if
(\Lambda_{2l_{i+1}}+x)\subset\Lambda
for any
x\in S_{i}(\theta^{*},E^{*})\cap\Lambda
relative to each ancestor
E_{i}\ (0\leq i\leq m-1)
of
E_{m}
. Moreover, we say that a set is
m
-strongly regular if
(\Lambda_{2l_{i+1}}+x)\subset\Lambda
for any
x\in S_{i}(\theta^{*},E^{*})
with
(\Lambda_{2l_{i+1}}+x)\cap\Lambda\neq\emptyset
relative to each ancestor
E_{i}\ (0\leq i\leq m-1)
of
E_{m}
.

Remark 3.8
.

We refer to Appendix
D
that we can always make an at most the present scale length-deformation on our blocks so that they are regular when required. Thus we can assume that all blocks are chosen to be regular. In fact, for a certain set
\Lambda
, the regularity condition implies that the whole blocks covering the previous-scale resonant sites in
\Lambda
are all contained in
\Lambda
, which ensures the resolvent identity iteration proceeds, and the nonresonant condition provides the resolvent bound for the blocks contained in
\Lambda
.

Finally, we say that a set is
m
-good if it is both
m
-nonresonant and
m
-regular. With the above definition, we have Green’s function estimates for
m
-good sets:

Fix
\theta^{*},E^{*}
and a finite set
\Lambda\subset\mathbb{Z}^{d}
. Let
E_{m}\in\mathcal{C}_{m}
be such that
E^{*}\in\tilde{J}(E_{m})
(if exists). If
\Lambda
is
m
-good, then for
|\theta-\theta^{*}|<\delta_{m}/(10D),|E-E^{*}|<\delta_{m}/5
,

\displaystyle\|G_{\Lambda}(\theta,E)\|

\displaystyle\leq 10\delta_{m}^{-1},

\displaystyle|G_{\Lambda}(\theta,E;x,y)|

\displaystyle\leq e^{-\gamma_{m}\|x-y\|_{1}},\ \|x-y\|_{1}\geq l_{m}^{\frac{5}{6}},

where
\gamma_{m}=(1-O(l_{m}^{-\frac{1}{30}}))\gamma_{m-1}\geq\frac{1}{2}\gamma_{0}
.
The above estimates also hold if
\Lambda
is
m
-regular and
E^{*}\notin\tilde{J}(E_{m})
for any
E_{m}\in\mathcal{C}_{m}
.

3.4.
Verification of induction hypotheses for
n=1

In Section
3.2
, we have verified Proposition
3.23
for
n=1
:

Hypothesis
1
by the construction.

Hypothesis
2
by Proposition
3.21
.

Hypothesis
3
by
3.8
(
j=1
) and Proposition
3.18
(
j=2
).

Hypothesis
4
by case analysis:

If
E_{1}^{(1)}\in\mathcal{C}_{1}^{(1)}
, then it belongs to
Type

1
or
2
(c.f. Proposition
3.6
,
3.10
). If the two brothers
E_{1,>}^{(2)},E_{1,<}^{(2)}\in\mathcal{C}_{1}^{(2)}
satisfy
