[\inf J(E_{n}),\sup J(E_{n+1})-\frac{1}{8}\delta_{n}]\subset(E_{n})_{\pm}(I(E_{n+1})_{\pm}).

Thus
\theta^{*}+x\cdot\omega\in I(E_{n+1})
.

Subcase B.

E_{n}
belongs to
Type

3
and
E_{n+1,<}
and
E_{n+1,>}
are the two Rellich children generated from the interval (
3.54
).

Since
E^{*}\in\tilde{J}(E_{n+1})
and
x\in S_{n}^{\Lambda}
, by the definition of
\tilde{J}(E_{n+1})
, we obtain

E_{n,\bullet}(\theta^{*}+x\cdot\omega)\in[\inf J(E_{n+1,<})+\frac{1}{8}\delta_{n},\sup J(E_{n+1,>})-\frac{1}{8}\delta_{n}]

(3.74)

for some
\bullet\in\{>,<\}
. If (
3.74
) holds for
\bullet=<
, by the approximation (
3.58
), it follows that

E_{n,<}(\theta^{*}+x\cdot\omega)\geq\inf J(E_{n+1,<})+\frac{1}{8}\delta_{n}\geq\inf(E_{n,<})_{\pm}(I(E_{n+1,<})),

and so
\theta^{*}+x\cdot\omega\in I(E_{n+1,<}).

Likewise, if (
3.74
) holds for
\bullet=>
, then
\theta^{*}+x\cdot\omega\in I(E_{n+1,>}).

Thus we finish the proof of the claim.

For
j=1
, we define
\bar{S}_{n}^{\Lambda}=S_{n}^{\Lambda}
.

For
Subcase A
, since
x\in S_{n}^{\Lambda}
,

|E_{n}(\theta^{*}+x\cdot\omega)-E^{*}|\leq\delta_{n},

and since
\Lambda
is
(n+1)
-nonresonant,

\delta_{n+1}\leq|E_{n+1}(\theta^{*}+x\cdot\omega)-E^{*}|.

For
Subcase B
, since
x\in S_{n}^{\Lambda}
,

\min_{\bullet\in\{>,<\}}|E_{n,\bullet}(\theta^{*}+x\cdot\omega)-E^{*}|\leq\delta_{n},

and since
\Lambda
is
(n+1)
-nonresonant,

\delta_{n+1}\leq\min_{\bullet\in\{>,<\}}|E_{n+1,\bullet}(\theta^{*}+x\cdot\omega)-E^{*}|.

By the eigenvalue separation estimates from Propositions
3.27
,
3.44
, it follows that
|\hat{E}-E^{*}|\geq\delta_{n+1}
for any eigenvalue
\hat{E}
of
H_{B_{n+1}^{(1)}+x}(\theta^{*})
, and so

\|G_{B_{n+1}^{(1)}+x}(\theta^{*},E^{*})\|\leq\delta_{n+1}^{-1}.

(2).

j=2
: Recalling that in double resonance case,
E_{n+1}
has a brother. We denote by
E_{n+1,>}
the larger one and
E_{n+1,<}
the smaller one. We prove that for any
x\in S_{n}^{\Lambda}
,
\theta^{*}+p\cdot\omega\in I(E_{n+1,<})\cup I(E_{n+1,>})
for some
p\in\{x,x-k_{n}\}
, where the definition of
k_{n}
is from (
3.37
).

Since
E^{*}\in\tilde{J}(E_{n+1})
and
x\in S_{n}^{\Lambda}
, by the definition of
\tilde{J}(E_{n+1})
, we obtain

E_{n}(\theta^{*}+x\cdot\omega)\in[\inf J(E_{n+1,<})+\frac{1}{8}\delta_{n},\sup J(E_{n+1,>})-\frac{1}{8}\delta_{n}].

Since
j=2
, by approximation (
3.52
), we have

[\inf J(E_{n+1,<})+\frac{1}{8}\delta_{n},\sup J(E_{n+1,>})-\frac{1}{8}\delta_{n}]\subset(E_{n})_{-}\left([\inf I(E_{n+1,>}),\sup I(E_{n+1,<})]\right)

and

[\inf J(E_{n+1,<})+\frac{1}{8}\delta_{n},\sup J(E_{n+1,>})-\frac{1}{8}\delta_{n}]\subset(E_{n})_{+}\left([\inf I(E_{n+1,<}),\sup I(E_{n+1,>})]+k_{n}\cdot\omega\right).

Thus

\theta^{*}+x\cdot\omega\in[\inf I(E_{n+1,>}),\sup I(E_{n+1,<})]\cup[\inf I(E_{n+1,<}),\sup I(E_{n+1,>})]+k_{n}\cdot\omega.

It follows that
\theta^{*}+x\cdot\omega\in[\inf I(E_{n+1,>}),\sup I(E_{n+1,<})]
or
\theta^{*}+(x-k_{n})\cdot\omega\in[\inf I(E_{n+1,<}),\sup I(E_{n+1,>})]
. Thus

\displaystyle\theta^{*}+p\cdot\omega

\displaystyle\in[\inf I(E_{n+1,>}),\sup I(E_{n+1,<})]\cup[\inf I(E_{n+1,<}),\sup I(E_{n+1,>})]

\displaystyle=I(E_{n+1,<})\cup I(E_{n+1,>})

for some
p\in\{x,x-k_{n}\}
. For
j=2
, we define
\bar{S}_{n}^{\Lambda}
to be the set of all points
p
coming from
x\in S_{n}^{\Lambda}
as above.
Since
\Lambda
is
(n+1)
-regular and
\|k_{n}\|_{1}\leq 10l_{n+1}^{(1)}<2l_{n+1}^{(2)}
, it follows that
\bar{S}_{n}^{\Lambda}\subset\Lambda
. Since
\Lambda
is
(n+1)
-nonresonant, by the eigenvalue separation estimates from Proposition
3.34
, it follows that
|\hat{E}-E^{*}|\geq\delta_{n+1}
for any eigenvalue
\hat{E}
of
H_{B_{n+1}^{(2)}+p}(\theta^{*})
, and so

\|G_{B_{n+1}^{(2)}+p}(\theta^{*},E^{*})\|\leq\delta_{n+1}^{-1}.

Now we assume that
E^{*}\notin\tilde{J}(E_{n+1})
for all
E_{n+1}\in\mathcal{C}(E_{n})
. Recall that
\tilde{\tilde{J}}(E_{n})
is almost covered by
\cup_{E_{n+1}\in\mathcal{C}(E_{n})}\tilde{J}(E_{n+1})
except the regions near a critical value of some
E_{n+1}\in\mathcal{C}(E_{n})
(c.f. Hypothesis
5
for
m=n+1
). Thus the

\theta\in\bigcup_{E_{n+1}\in\mathcal{C}(E_{n})}I(E_{n+1})

minimizing

\operatorname{dist}(E^{*},E_{n+1}(\theta)),\ E_{n+1}\in\mathcal{C}(E_{n})

must attain at a critical point of some
E_{n+1}\in\mathcal{C}^{(j)}(E_{n})
. By the same argument as above, it follows that for this
E_{n+1}
,

\theta^{*}+p\cdot\omega\in I(E_{n+1})
for some
p\in\{x,x-k_{n}\}
. By the definition of
\tilde{J}(E_{n+1})
,
we have

\delta_{n+1}\leq|E_{n+1}(\theta^{*}+p\cdot\omega)-E^{*}|.

Thus we also have

\|G_{B_{n+1}^{(j)}+p}(\theta^{*},E^{*})\|\leq\delta_{n+1}^{-1}.

For both
j=1,2
, by the
(n+1)
-regularity of
\Lambda
,
(B_{n+1}+p)\subset\Lambda
for all
p\in\bar{S}_{n}^{\Lambda}
. Moreover, by Diophantine condition, the separation of
p\in\bar{S}_{n}^{\Lambda}
is larger than the size of
B_{n+1}
, thus the blocks
\{B_{n+1}+p\}_{p\in\bar{S}_{n}^{\Lambda}}
are non-overlapping.

Next, we prove the case that
\Lambda=B_{n+1}+p
is a single
(n+1)
-nonresonant block. By the previous discussion, we have

\|G_{\Lambda}(\theta^{*},E^{*})\|\leq\delta_{n+1}^{-1}.

It follows that by Neumann series argument, for
|\theta-\theta^{*}|<\delta_{n+1}/(10D)
and
|E-E^{*}|<\frac{2}{5}\delta_{n+1}
,

\|G_{\Lambda}(\theta,E)\|\leq 2\delta_{n+1}^{-1}.

Let
x,y\in\Lambda
satisfy
\|x-y\|_{1}\geq l_{n+1}^{\frac{4}{5}}
. Since
G_{\Lambda}
is self-adjoint, we may assume
\|x-p\|_{1}\geq l_{n+1}^{\frac{3}{4}}
. Let
\Omega_{n+1,p}
be an
l_{n+1}^{\frac{2}{3}}
-size block centered at
p
such that
\Lambda\setminus\Omega_{n+1,p}
is
n
-good. Recalling the estimates for
n
-good sets from Hypothesis
6
, by resolvent identity, we obtain

\displaystyle|G_{\Lambda}(\theta,E;x,y)|

\displaystyle=|G_{\Lambda\setminus\Omega_{n+1,p}}(x,y)\chi(y)+\sum_{z,z^{\prime}}G_{\Lambda\setminus\Omega_{n+1,p}}(x,z)\Gamma_{z,z^{\prime}}G_{\Lambda}(z^{\prime},y)|

\displaystyle\leq e^{-\gamma_{n}\|x-y\|_{1}}+C(d)\sup_{z,z^{\prime}}e^{-\gamma_{n}\|x-z\|_{1}}|G_{\Lambda}(z^{\prime},y)|

\displaystyle\leq e^{-\gamma_{n}\|x-y\|_{1}}+C(d)\sup_{z,z^{\prime}}e^{-\gamma_{n}\|x-z\|_{1}}e^{-\gamma_{n}(\|z^{\prime}-y\|_{1}-l_{n+1}^{\frac{3}{4}})}\delta_{n+1}^{-1}

\displaystyle\leq e^{-\gamma^{\prime}_{n}\|x-y\|_{1}}

with
\gamma^{\prime}_{n}=(1-O(l_{n+1}^{-\frac{1}{30}}))\gamma_{n}
, where we have used if
\|z^{\prime}-y\|_{1}\leq l_{n+1}^{\frac{3}{4}}
, then

|G_{\Lambda}(z^{\prime},y)|\leq\|G_{\Lambda}\|\leq 2\delta_{n+1}^{-1}\leq 2e^{-\gamma_{n}(\|z^{\prime}-y\|_{1}-l_{n+1}^{\frac{3}{4}})}\delta_{n+1}^{-1},

if
\|z^{\prime}-y\|_{1}\geq l_{n+1}^{\frac{3}{4}}
, then

\displaystyle|G_{\Lambda}(z^{\prime},y)|=|G_{\Lambda}(y,z^{\prime})|

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{w,w^{\prime}}|G_{\Lambda\setminus\Omega_{n+1,p}}(y,w)\Gamma_{w,w^{\prime}}G_{\Lambda}(w^{\prime},z^{\prime})|

\displaystyle\leq C(d)e^{-\gamma_{n}\|y-w\|_{1}}\|G_{\Lambda}\|

\displaystyle\leq C(d)e^{-\gamma_{n}(\|y-z^{\prime}\|_{1}-l_{n+1}^{\frac{3}{4}})}\delta_{n+1}^{-1}

and
\delta_{n+1}^{-1}=e^{l_{n+1}^{\frac{2}{3}}}\ll e^{\gamma_{n}\|x-y\|_{1}}
to bound the second term. Thus we finish the case that
\Lambda
is a single
(n+1)
-nonresonant block.

Now, assume
\Lambda
is an arbitrary
(n+1)
-good set. We must show that
G_{\Lambda}
dose exist. By Schur’s test, it suffices to prove

\sup_{x}\sum_{y}|G_{\Lambda}(\theta,E+io;x,y)|<C<\infty.

(3.75)

Define

\Lambda^{\prime}:=\Lambda\setminus\bigcup_{p\in\bar{S}_{n}^{\Lambda}}\Omega_{n+1,p}.

Then
\Lambda^{\prime}
is
n
-nonresonant since
S_{n}^{\Lambda}\subset\cup_{p\in\bar{S}_{n}^{\Lambda}}\Omega_{n+1,p}
and is
n
-regular since
\Lambda
is
(n+1)
-regular and each
(B_{n+1}+p)\setminus\Omega_{n+1,p}
is
n
-regular. Let
\tilde{\Omega}_{n+1,p}
be a
2l_{n+1}^{\frac{2}{3}}
-size cube centered at
p
. For
x\in\Lambda\setminus\cup_{p\in\bar{S}_{n}^{\Lambda}}\tilde{\Omega}_{n+1,p}
, by resolvent identity, we have

\displaystyle\sum_{y}|G_{\Lambda}(x,y)|

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{y}|G_{\Lambda^{\prime}}(x,y)|+\sum_{z,z^{\prime},y}|G_{\Lambda^{\prime}}(x,z)\Gamma_{z,z^{\prime}}G_{\Lambda}(z^{\prime},y)|

\displaystyle\leq C(d)\delta_{n}^{-2}+C(d)e^{-l_{n+1}^{\frac{2}{3}}}\sup_{z^{\prime}}\sum_{y}|G_{\Lambda}(z^{\prime},y)|.

For
x\in\tilde{\Omega}_{n+1,p}
, by resolvent identity, we have

\displaystyle\sum_{y}|G_{\Lambda}(x,y)|

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{y}|G_{B_{n+1}+p}(x,y)|+\sum_{z,z^{\prime},y}|G_{B_{n+1}+p}(x,z)\Gamma_{z,z^{\prime}}G_{\Lambda}(z^{\prime},y)|

\displaystyle\leq\delta_{n+1}^{-2}+C(d)e^{-\frac{1}{2}l_{n+1}}\sup_{z^{\prime}}\sum_{y}|G_{\Lambda}(z^{\prime},y)|.

Taking supremum for
x
on the left hand side of the above two inequalities, we get

\sup_{x}\sum_{y}|G_{\Lambda}(x,y)|\leq\delta_{n+1}^{-2}+\frac{1}{2}\sup_{x}\sum_{y}|G_{\Lambda}(x,y)|,

thus

\sup_{x}\sum_{y}|G_{\Lambda}(x,y)|\leq 2\delta_{n+1}^{-2},

which gives (
3.75
).
Since for
|\theta-\theta^{*}|<\delta_{n+1}/(10D)
and
|E-E^{*}|<\frac{2}{5}\delta_{n+1}
,
G_{\Lambda}(\theta,E)
does exist, we obtain
\operatorname{dist}(\sigma(H_{\Lambda}(\theta)),E^{*})\geq\frac{2}{5}\delta_{n+1}
and hence
\operatorname{dist}(\sigma(H_{\Lambda}(\theta)),E)\geq\frac{1}{5}\delta_{n+1}
for all
|E-E^{*}|<\frac{1}{5}\delta_{n+1}
. Thus we get the desired operator norm

\|G_{\Lambda}(\theta,E)\|=\|\left(H_{\Lambda}(\theta)-E\right)^{-1}\|=\frac{1}{\operatorname{dist}(\sigma(H_{\Lambda}(\theta)),E)}\leq 10\delta_{n+1}^{-1}.

It remains to prove the off-diagonal decay of
G_{\Lambda}
.
Let
x,y\in\Lambda
satisfy
\|x-y\|_{1}\geq l_{n+1}^{\frac{5}{6}}
. We define

B_{x}:=\left\{\begin{aligned} &\Lambda_{\lambda_{1}^{\frac{1}{2}}}(x)\cap\Lambda\quad\text{if }x\in\Lambda\setminus\bigcup_{p\in\bar{S}_{0}^{\Lambda}}\tilde{\Omega}_{1,p},\\
&B_{m}+p_{m}\ \quad\text{if }x\in\tilde{\Omega}_{m,p_{m}},\end{aligned}\right.

where
p_{m}\in\bar{S}_{m-1}^{\Lambda}
and
m
satisfying
1\leq m\leq n+1
is the smallest integer such that
p_{m}\notin S_{m}(\theta^{*},E^{*})
.
The set
B_{x}
has the following two properties:
(1).

B_{x}
is
m
-good for some
0\leq m\leq n+1
;
(2).

x
is closed to the center of
B_{x}
and away from its relative boundary
\partial_{\Lambda}B_{x}
. We can iterate the resolvent identity to obtain

\displaystyle|G_{\Lambda}(x,y)|

\displaystyle\leq\prod_{s=0}^{L-1}(C(d)l_{m_{s}}^{d}e^{-\gamma_{m_{s}-1}^{\prime}\|x_{s}-x_{s+1}\|_{1}})|G_{\Lambda}(x_{L},y)|

\displaystyle\leq e^{-\gamma_{n}^{\prime\prime}\|x-x_{L}\|_{1}}|G_{\Lambda}(x_{L},y)|,

(3.76)

where
x_{0}:=x
,
x_{s+1}\in\partial B_{x_{s}}
with
\|x_{s}-x_{s+1}\|_{1}\geq\frac{1}{2}l_{m_{s}}\gg l_{m_{s}}^{\frac{5}{6}}
. We stop the iteration until
y\in B_{x_{L}}
.
By resolvent identity again, we get

\displaystyle|G_{\Lambda}(x_{L},y)|

\displaystyle\leq|G_{B_{x_{L}}}(x_{L},y)|+\sum_{z,z^{\prime}}|G_{B_{x_{L}}}(x_{L},z)\Gamma_{z,z^{\prime}}G_{\Lambda}(z^{\prime},y)|

\displaystyle\leq C(d)e^{-\gamma_{n}^{\prime}(\|x_{L}-y\|_{1}-l_{n+1}^{\frac{4}{5}})}\delta_{n+1}^{-1},

(3.77)

where we have used the off-diagonal exponential decay of
G_{B_{x_{L}}}
and
\|G_{\Lambda}\|\leq 10\delta_{n+1}^{-1}
. Since
\|x-y\|_{1}\geq l_{n+1}^{\frac{5}{6}}
, (
3.76
) and (
3.77
) give the desired off-diagonal estimate

|G_{\Lambda}(x,y)|\leq e^{-\gamma_{n+1}\|x-y\|_{1}}

with
\gamma_{n+1}=(1-O(l_{n+1}^{-\frac{1}{30}}))\gamma_{n}
.

Thus we finish the proof.
∎

4.
Proof of main Theorem

In this section, we will relate the Rellich functions we have constructed in the previous section to spectral and dynamical properties of
H
and give the proof of Theorem
1.1
.

4.1.
Anderson localization

We first construct the set where Anderson localization can occur.

For each function
E_{n}\in\mathcal{C}_{n}\ (n\geq 1)
, noticing that
\delta_{n-2}^{10}\ll(l_{n+2})^{-2\tau}
, one can employ the argument from the proof of Lemma
3.24
to show that there exists at most one
\tilde{k}_{n}
with
0\neq\|{\tilde{k}_{n}}\|_{1}\leq 10l_{n+2}
, such that there is some
\theta_{{\tilde{k}_{n}},-}\in I(E_{n})_{-}
satisfying

\theta_{{\tilde{k}_{n}},-}+{\tilde{k}_{n}}\cdot\omega\in I(E_{n})_{+},\ e_{\tilde{k}_{n}}:=E_{n}(\theta_{{\tilde{k}_{n}},-})=E_{n}(\theta_{{\tilde{k}_{n}},-}+{\tilde{k}_{n}}\cdot\omega).

Moreover, if such
\tilde{k}_{n}
exists, then
E_{n}
belongs to
Type

1
.
If the above
\tilde{k}_{n}
exists, we define

J^{DR}(E_{n}):=B_{\delta_{n-1}^{10}}(e_{\tilde{k}_{n}}).

(4.1)

Now define

K_{n}:=\bigcup_{E_{n}\in\mathcal{C}_{n}}\bigcup_{\|k\|_{1}\leq l_{n+1}}(E_{n}^{-1}(J^{DR}(E_{n}))+k\cdot\omega)

(4.2)

and

K:=\bigcap_{s\geq 1}\bigcup_{n\geq s}K_{n}.

(4.3)

Denote by
|\cdot|
the Lebesgue measure of a set. We have

Lemma 4.1
.

|K|=0.

Proof.

By construction (c.f.
(5)
of Proposition
3.25
), for any
E_{s}\in\mathcal{C}_{s}
,
|\mathcal{C}(E_{s})|\leq 2\delta_{s-1}^{-10}
. Thus for
n\geq 1
,

|\mathcal{C}_{n}|\leq\prod_{s=0}^{n-1}(2\delta_{s-1}^{-10})\leq\delta_{n-2}^{-11}.

(4.4)

Fix
E_{n}\in\mathcal{C}_{n}
. Since each
|J^{DR}(E_{n})|=2\delta_{n-1}^{10}
and
E_{n}
satisfies the Morse condition with the two-monotonicity interval structure, we can employ Lemma
2.3
to obtain

|E_{n}^{-1}(J^{DR}(E_{n}))|\leq 2\sqrt{2|J^{DR}(E_{n})|}\leq 4\delta_{n-1}^{5}.

(4.5)

Combining the estimates (
4.4
) and (
4.5
), we get

|K_{n}|\lesssim\delta_{n-2}^{-11}(l_{n+1})^{d}\delta_{n-1}^{5}\leq\delta_{n-1}

and then

\sum_{n=1}^{+\infty}|K_{n}|<+\infty.

Thus by Borel-Cantelli Lemma,
|K|=0
.
∎

Thus
\Theta:=\mathbb{T}\setminus K
is a set of full measure. For the remainder of this section, we fix
\theta\in\Theta
. Thus there exists some
N_{1}
such that
\theta\notin K_{n}
for all
n\geq N_{1}
.

To prove
H(\theta)
has Anderson localization using Schnol’s lemma, it suffices to show that every generalized eigenfunction
\psi
of
H(\theta)
that grows at most polynomially (i.e.,
|\psi(x)|\leq(1+\|x\|_{1})^{d}
) in fact decays exponentially. For the remainder of this section, we fix a generalized eigenvalue
E
and its generalized eigenfunction
\psi
.

Lemma 4.2
.

There exists some
N_{2}
such that for all
n\geq N_{2}
,
E\in\tilde{J}(E_{n})
for some
E_{n}\in\mathcal{C}_{n}
. Moreover, there exists an
x_{n}\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}
satisfying
\|x_{n}\|_{1}\leq 100l_{n}
such that
x_{n}\in S_{n}(\theta,E)
relative to this
E_{n}
.

Proof.

If not, there exists an increasing integer sequence
\{n_{i}\}_{i=0}^{\infty}
such that
\Lambda_{100l_{n_{i}}}
is
n_{i}
-nonresonant and
\{U_{i}\}_{i=0}^{\infty}
such that
U_{i}
is
n_{i}
-regular and satisfies

\Lambda_{50l_{n_{i}}}\subset U_{i}\subset\Lambda_{100l_{n_{i}}}.

Thus
U_{i}
satisfies the condition of Hypothesis
6
at scale
n_{i}
. It follows that
G_{U_{i}}(\theta,E;x,y)
decays exponentially for
\|x-y\|_{1}\geq l_{n_{i}}^{\frac{5}{6}}
. For any
\|x\|_{1}\leq\frac{1}{3}n_{i}
, one has
\operatorname{dist}(x,\partial U_{i})\geq 4l_{n_{i}}>l_{n_{i}}^{\frac{5}{6}}
. Thus applying the Poisson formula gives

|\psi(x)|\leq\sum_{z,z^{\prime}}|G_{U_{i}}(x,z)\Gamma_{z,z^{\prime}}\psi(z^{\prime})|\lesssim l_{n_{i}}^{2d}e^{-2\gamma_{0}l_{n_{i}}}\leq e^{-\gamma_{0}l_{n_{i}}}.

Fixing
x\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}
, we have
\|x\|_{1}\leq\frac{1}{3}n_{i}
, as
i\to\infty
. Thus we must have
\psi(x)=0
by letting
i\to\infty
, a contradiction since
\psi\neq 0
.
∎

We are ready to prove Anderson localization.

Proof of Anderson localization.

First, we note that fixing
E_{n}\in\mathcal{C}_{n}
, if
E_{n}
belongs to
Type

3
, then for any
\theta\in I(E_{n,>})\cup I(E_{n,<})
and
0<\|y\|_{1}\leq 10l_{n+2}
, we have
\theta+y\cdot\omega\notin I(E_{n,>})\cup I(E_{n,<})
since
I(E_{n,>})\cup I(E_{n,<})
is a single interval with length
\ll l_{n+2}^{-\tau}
, and if
\theta\in I(E_{n})\setminus E_{n}^{-1}(J^{DR}(E_{n}))
, then
\theta
is in the simple resonant interval. An analogous argument in the proof of Proposition
3.26
yields that for
0<\|y\|_{1}\leq 10l_{n+2}
with
\theta+y\cdot\omega\in I(E_{n})
,

|E_{n}(\theta)-E_{n}(\theta+y\cdot\omega)|\geq 3\delta_{n}.

(4.6)

Let
n\geq\max(N_{1},N_{2}):=N
. By Lemma
4.2
and the definition of
S_{n}(\theta,E)
, there exists some
x_{n}
satisfying
\|x_{n}\|_{1}\leq 100l_{n}\leq l_{n+1}
, such that
\theta+x_{n}\cdot\omega\in I(E_{n})
and

|E_{n}(\theta+x_{n}\cdot\omega)-E|\leq\delta_{n}.

Since
\theta\notin K_{n}
, by the definition of
K_{n}
, we have
\theta+x_{n}\cdot\omega\notin E_{n}^{-1}(J^{DR}(E_{n}))
. Thus by (
4.6
) (with
\theta+x_{n}\cdot\omega
in the role of
\theta
), for any
x
with
0<\|x-x_{n}\|_{1}\leq 10l_{n+2}
and
\theta+x\cdot\omega\in I(E_{n})
,

|E_{n}(\theta+x\cdot\omega)-E|\geq|E_{n}(\theta+x\cdot\omega)-E_{n}(\theta+x_{n}\cdot\omega)|-|E_{n}(\theta+x_{n}\cdot\omega)-E|\geq\delta_{n}.

It follows that the annulus
\Lambda_{9l_{n+2}}\setminus\Lambda_{l_{n+1}}
is
n
-nonresonant. Thus there exists an
n
-good set
\Lambda
, which is a deformation of
\Lambda_{8l_{n+2}}\setminus\Lambda_{2l_{n+1}}
satisfying

(\Lambda_{8l_{n+2}}\setminus\Lambda_{2l_{n+1}})\subset\Lambda\subset(\Lambda_{9l_{n+2}}\setminus\Lambda_{l_{n+1}}).

Let
x\in\Lambda_{6l_{n+2}}\setminus\Lambda_{4l_{n+1}}.
Then

\operatorname{dist}(x,\partial\Lambda)\geq\frac{1}{5}\|x\|_{1}>l_{n}^{\frac{5}{6}}.

Thus

\displaystyle|\psi(x)|

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{z,z^{\prime}}|G_{\Lambda}(x,z)\Gamma_{z,z^{\prime}}\psi(z^{\prime})|

\displaystyle\lesssim(l_{n+2})^{2d}e^{-\frac{1}{10}\gamma_{0}\|x\|_{1}}

\displaystyle\leq e^{-\frac{1}{20}\gamma_{0}\|x\|_{1}}.

Since

\left(\mathbb{Z}^{d}\setminus\bigcup_{n\geq N}(\Lambda_{6l_{n+2}}\setminus\Lambda_{4l_{n+1}})\right)\subset\Lambda_{4l_{N+1}},

the above estimate shows the exponential decay of
\psi(x)
for
\|x\|_{1}>4l_{N+1}
.

We finish the proof of Anderson localization for
\theta\in\Theta
.
∎

4.2.
Dynamical localization

We first construct the set where dynamical localization can occur.

Fix
E_{n}\in\mathcal{C}_{n}\ (n\geq 1)
. Recalling the definition of
J^{DR}(E_{n})
in the previous section (c.f. (
4.1
)), we define analogously
J^{DR}_{\rho}
for
\rho>0
,

J^{DR}_{\rho}(E_{n}):=B_{\rho\delta_{n-1}^{9}}(\tilde{k}_{n}),

and

K_{\rho}:=\bigcup_{n\geq 1}\bigcup_{E_{n}\in\mathcal{C}_{n}}\bigcup_{\|k\|_{1}\leq l_{n+1}}(E_{n}^{-1}(J^{DR}_{\rho}(E_{n}))+k\cdot\omega).

As the proof of Lemma
4.1
, one can prove
|K_{\rho}|\leq\rho^{\frac{1}{2}}.
Recalling the set
K
(c.f. (
4.3
)), we have
K\subset K_{\rho}
for any
\rho>0
. Thus Anderson localization holds for
\theta\in\mathbb{T}\setminus K_{\rho}
by the previous theorem. We will show dynamical localization also occurs on this set.

For the remainder of this section, we fix
\theta\in\mathbb{T}\setminus K_{\rho}
and denote by
\{\lambda_{\alpha},\varphi_{\alpha}\}_{\alpha\in\mathbb{N}}
the complete set of eigenvalues and corresponding eigenfunctions of
H(\theta)
.
Then

{\bm{e}}_{0}=\sum_{\alpha}\varphi_{\alpha}(0)\varphi_{\alpha},

where

\varphi_{\alpha}(0)=\langle\varphi_{\alpha},{\bm{e}}_{0}\rangle.

Hence

e^{itH(\theta)}{\bm{e}}_{0}=\sum_{\alpha}e^{it\lambda_{\alpha}}\varphi_{\alpha}(0)\varphi_{\alpha}.

To control

\sum_{x\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}}(1+\|x\|_{1})^{q}|\langle e^{itH(\theta)}{\bm{e}}_{0},{\bm{e}}_{x}\rangle|,

it suffices to estimate

\sum_{\alpha}\left(\sum_{x}(1+\|x\|_{1})^{q}|\varphi_{\alpha}(x)|\right)|\varphi_{\alpha}(0)|.

Let
A_{0}=\emptyset
and
A_{j}=\{\alpha:\ |\varphi_{\alpha}(0)|>e^{-\gamma_{0}l_{j}}\}\ (j\geq 1).

Then we can rewrite the above summation as

\sum_{j=1}^{+\infty}\sum_{\alpha\in A_{j}\setminus A_{j-1}}\left(\sum_{x}(1+\|x\|_{1})^{q}|\varphi_{\alpha}(x)|\right)|\varphi_{\alpha}(0)|.

(4.7)

Lemma 4.3
.

Let
\alpha\in A_{j}
. Then for all
n\geq j
, there exists some
E_{n}\in\mathcal{C}_{n}
such that
\lambda_{\alpha}\in\tilde{J}(E_{n})
. Moreover, there exists an
x_{n}\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}
satisfying
\|x_{n}\|_{1}\leq 100l_{n}
such that
x_{n}\in S_{n}(\theta,\lambda_{\alpha})
relative to this
E_{n}
.

Proof.

The proof is similar to that of Lemma
4.2
.
∎

Let
m\geq 1
be the smallest integer such that
\rho\geq\delta_{m-1}
. From the above lemma, as in the proof of Anderson localization (via the Poisson formula argument), one can prove that for
\alpha\in A_{j},x\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}
satisfying
\|x\|_{1}>4\max(l_{m+1},l_{j+1}):=L_{\rho,j}
,

|\varphi_{\alpha}(x)|\leq e^{-\frac{1}{20}\gamma_{0}\|x\|_{1}}.

By the exponential decay of the eigenfunction, we employ the Hilbert-Schmidt argument to obtain

\displaystyle L_{\rho,j}^{d}

\displaystyle\gtrsim\sum_{\|x\|_{1}\leq L_{\rho,j}}\sum_{\alpha}|\varphi_{\alpha}(x)|^{2}

\displaystyle\geq\sum_{\alpha\in A_{j}}\sum_{\|x\|_{1}\leq L_{\rho,j}}|\varphi_{\alpha}(x)|^{2}

\displaystyle=\sum_{\alpha\in A_{j}}(1-\sum_{\|x\|_{1}>L_{\rho,j}}|\varphi_{\alpha}(x)|^{2})

\displaystyle\geq\frac{1}{2}|A_{j}|.

Thus
|A_{j}|\lesssim L_{\rho,j}^{d}
.

Using the estimate
|\varphi_{\alpha}(x)|\leq e^{-\frac{1}{20}\gamma_{0}\|x\|_{1}}
for
\alpha\in A_{m}
and
\|x\|_{1}>L_{\rho,m}=4l_{m+1}
, we get

\displaystyle\sum_{j=1}^{m}

\displaystyle\sum_{\alpha\in A_{j}\setminus A_{j-1}}\left(\sum_{x}(1+\|x\|_{1})^{q}|\varphi_{\alpha}(x)|\right)|\varphi_{\alpha}(0)|\leq\sum_{\alpha\in A_{m}}\left(\sum_{x}(1+\|x\|_{1})^{q}|\varphi_{\alpha}(x)|\right)

\displaystyle\leq|A_{m}|\sup_{\alpha\in A_{m}}\left(\sum_{\|x\|_{1}\leq 4l_{m+1}}+\sum_{\|x\|_{1}>4l_{m+1}}\right)(1+\|x\|_{1})^{q}|\varphi_{\alpha}(x)|

\displaystyle\leq C({q,d})l_{m+1}^{q+2d}.

(4.8)

Since
|\varphi_{\alpha}(0)|\leq e^{-\gamma_{0}l_{j-1}}
for
\alpha\notin A_{j-1}
, using the estimate
|\varphi_{\alpha}(x)|\leq e^{-\frac{1}{20}\gamma_{0}\|x\|_{1}}
for
\alpha\in A_{j}\ (j>m)
and
\|x\|_{1}>L_{\rho,j}=4l_{j+1}
, we get

\displaystyle\ \ \ \sum_{\alpha\in A_{j}\setminus A_{j-1}}\left(\sum_{x}(1+\|x\|_{1})^{q}|\varphi_{\alpha}(x)|\right)|\varphi_{\alpha}(0)|

\displaystyle\leq|A_{j}|e^{-\gamma_{0}l_{j-1}}\sup_{\alpha\in A_{j}}\left(\sum_{\|x\|_{1}\leq 4l_{j+1}}+\sum_{\|x\|_{1}>4l_{j+1}}\right)(1+\|x\|_{1})^{q}|\varphi_{\alpha}(x)|

\displaystyle\leq C(q,d)l_{j+1}^{q+2d}e^{-\gamma_{0}l_{j-1}}.

Summing up the above estimates for
j
from
m+1
to
+\infty
implies

\displaystyle\sum_{j=m+1}^{+\infty}\sum_{\alpha\in A_{j}\setminus A_{j-1}}\left(\sum_{x}(1+\|x\|_{1})^{q}|\varphi_{\alpha}(x)|\right)|\varphi_{\alpha}(0)|\leq C(q,d)e^{-\frac{1}{2}\gamma_{0}l_{m}}.

(4.9)

From (
4.2
) and (
4.9
), we obtain

\displaystyle\eqref{kuai}

\displaystyle\leq C({q,d})l_{m+1}^{q+2d}

(4.10)

\displaystyle\leq C({q,d})\max(|\log\delta_{m-2}|^{100(q+2d)},l_{2}^{q+2d})

\displaystyle\leq C({q,d})\max(|\log\rho|,|\log\varepsilon_{0}|)^{100(q+2d)}<+\infty,

where on the third line, we use
\rho\leq\delta_{m-2}
for
m\geq 2
by the definition of
m
. Hence we finish proving
H(\theta)
satisfies dynamical localization on the set
\mathbb{T}\setminus K_{\rho}
for all
\rho>0
. Since
|K_{\rho}|\leq\rho^{\frac{1}{2}}
, it follows that dynamical localization holds for Lebesgue almost
\theta\in\mathbb{T}
.

Next, we prove the expectation version of dynamical localization. For this purpose, we employ the estimate from (
4.10
) to get

\displaystyle\ \ \ \left(\int_{\mathbb{T}\setminus K_{\delta_{1}}}+\sum_{n=1}^{+\infty}\int_{K_{\delta_{n}}\setminus K_{\delta_{n+1}}}\right)\sup_{t\in\mathbb{R}}\sum_{x\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}}(1+\|x\|_{1})^{q}|\langle e^{itH(\theta)}{\bm{e}}_{0},{\bm{e}}_{x}\rangle|d\theta

\displaystyle\leq C({q,d})\left(|\log\delta_{1}|^{100(q+2d)}+\sum_{n=1}^{+\infty}|\log\delta_{n+1}|^{100(q+2d)}|K_{\delta_{n}}|\right)

\displaystyle<+\infty.

4.3.
(\frac{1}{2}-)
-Hölder regularity of IDS

In this section,
we fix
\theta\in\mathbb{T},E\in\mathbb{R}
and
\eta>0
. We are going to estimate the number of eigenvalues of
H_{\Lambda_{N}}(\theta)
belonging to
[E-\eta,E+\eta]
as
N\to+\infty
.
For this purpose, we first introduce a lemma relating the operator norm of Green’s function to the number of eigenvalues inside the energy interval
[E-\eta,E+\eta]
.

Lemma 4.4
.

Let
H
be a self-adjoint operator on
\mathbb{Z}^{d}
and
\Lambda\subset\mathbb{Z}^{d}
be a finite set. Fix
E\in\mathbb{R}
. Assume there exists a set
\Lambda^{\prime}\subset\mathbb{Z}^{d}
such that
|\Lambda\Delta\Lambda^{\prime}|\leq M
and
\|G_{\Lambda^{\prime}}(E)\|\leq(2\eta)^{-1}
, where
G_{\Lambda^{\prime}}(E)=(H_{\Lambda^{\prime}}-E)^{-1}
. Then the number of eigenvalues of
H_{\Lambda}
inside
[E-\eta,E+\eta]
does not exceed
3M
.

Remark 4.1
.

This lemma only works for self-adjoint matrices.

Proof.

We refer to
[
CSZ23
]
for the detailed proof (c.f. page 553 of
[
CSZ23
]
).

∎

Let
N
be sufficiently large depending on
\eta
. The proof proceeds by case analysis:

(1).

\varepsilon_{0}=\delta_{0}^{20}<\eta
.

We define

S_{\eta}:=\{x\in\Lambda_{N}:\ |v(\theta+x\cdot\omega)-E|\leq(2d+2)\eta\}.

Since
v
has a two-monotonicity interval structure and satisfies the Morse condition,

|v^{-1}\left([E-(2d+2)\eta,E+(2d+2)\eta]\right)|\leq C(d)\eta^{\frac{1}{2}}.

From the uniform distribution of
\{x\cdot\omega\}_{x\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}}
, we deduce that
|S_{\eta}|\leq C(d)\eta^{\frac{1}{2}}|\Lambda_{N}|
for sufficiently large
N
. Let
\Lambda^{\prime}=\Lambda_{N}\setminus S_{\eta}
. Then by Neumann series argument,

\|G_{\Lambda^{\prime}}(E;\theta)\|\leq((2d+2)\eta-\varepsilon\|\Delta\|)^{-1}\leq((2d+2)\eta-2d\varepsilon)^{-1}\leq(2\eta)^{-1}.

By Lemma
4.4
,
H_{\Lambda_{N}}(\theta)
has at most
C(d)\eta^{\frac{1}{2}}|\Lambda_{N}|
eigenvalues in
[E-\eta,E+\eta]
. Thus

\mathcal{N}_{\Lambda_{N}}(E+\eta;\theta)-\mathcal{N}_{\Lambda_{N}}(E-\eta;\theta)\leq C(d)\eta^{\frac{1}{2}}.

(2).

\eta\in(\delta_{n+1}^{20},\delta_{n}^{20}]
for some
n\geq 0
.

Replacing
\delta_{n+1}
with
\eta
in the proof of Theorem
3.48
where we essentially use the relation
|\log\delta_{n}|\ll|\log\delta_{n+1}|\ll l_{n+1}
, one can obtain the following lemma:

Lemma 4.5
.

Let
E_{n+1}\in\mathcal{C}_{n+1}
be such that
E\in\tilde{J}(E_{n+1})
or
E_{n+1}
minimizes
\operatorname{dist}(\tilde{J}(\textbf{E}),E)
among
\textbf{E}\in\mathcal{C}_{n+1}
. Let
\Lambda\subset\mathbb{Z}^{d}
be an
(n+1)
-regular set satisfying

|E_{n+1}(\theta+x\cdot\omega)-E|\geq 20\eta

for any
x\in\Lambda
with
\theta+x\cdot\omega\in I(E_{n+1})
. Then

\|G_{\Lambda}(\theta,E)\|\leq\frac{1}{2}\eta^{-1}.

We find an
(n+1)
-regular set
\Lambda
such that

\Lambda_{N}\subset\Lambda\subset\Lambda_{N+50l_{n+1}}

and define

S_{\eta}:=\{x\in\Lambda:\ \theta+x\cdot\omega\in I(E_{n+1}),\ |E_{n+1}(\theta+x\cdot\omega)-E|<20\eta\}.

Then the set
\Lambda^{\prime}:=\Lambda\setminus(\bigcup_{x\in S_{\eta}}B_{n+1}+x)
satisfies the assumption of Lemma
4.5
and so

\|G_{\Lambda^{\prime}}(\theta,E)\|\leq\frac{1}{2}\eta^{-1}.

(4.11)

As done above, we have for sufficiently large
N
,

|S_{\eta}|\leq C(d)\eta^{\frac{1}{2}}|\Lambda_{N+50l_{n+1}}|\leq C(d)\eta^{\frac{1}{2}}|\Lambda_{N}|.

It follows that

\displaystyle|\Lambda_{N}\setminus\Lambda^{\prime}|+|\Lambda^{\prime}\setminus\Lambda_{N}|

\displaystyle\leq|\Lambda\setminus\Lambda^{\prime}|+|\Lambda\setminus\Lambda_{N}|

(4.12)

\displaystyle\leq C(d)(l_{n+1}^{d}|S_{\eta}|+l_{n+1}N^{d-1})

\displaystyle\leq C(d)(l_{n+1}^{d}\eta^{\frac{1}{2}}|\Lambda_{N}|+l_{n+1}N^{d-1}).

Combining (
4.11
), (
4.12
) and Lemma
4.4
implies

\displaystyle\mathcal{N}_{\Lambda_{N}}(E+\eta;\theta)-\mathcal{N}_{\Lambda_{N}}(E-\eta;\theta)

\displaystyle\leq C(d)(l_{n+1}^{d}\eta^{\frac{1}{2}}+l_{n+1}/N)

\displaystyle\leq C(d)\eta^{\frac{1}{2}}|\log\eta|^{16d}

provided
N
sufficiently large, where we use

l_{n+1}\leq l_{n}^{8}\leq|\log\delta_{n}|^{16}\leq|\log\eta|^{16}

on the second line.

We finish the proof by combining the above two cases and letting
N\to+\infty
.

Appendix A
Proof of Lemma
2.1

Proof.

Without loss of generality, we may assume
E^{*}=0
. It suffices to show the smallest
m
eigenvalues of the positive semidefinite operator
H^{2}
,
0\leq\lambda_{1}\leq\cdots\leq\lambda_{m}
satisfy

\sum_{k=1}^{m}\lambda_{k}\leq m\delta^{2}.

Denote by
P
the orthogonal projection on the space spanned by
\psi_{k}\ (1\leq k\leq m)
. Thus the compressed operator
PH^{2}P
has
m
eigenvalues
0\leq\mu_{1}\leq\cdots\leq\mu_{m}
satisfying
\lambda_{k}\leq\mu_{k}
by min-max principle. Thus we obtain

\displaystyle\sum_{k=1}^{m}\lambda_{k}\leq\sum_{k=1}^{m}\mu_{k}

\displaystyle=\operatorname{Trace}(PH^{2}P)

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{k=1}^{m}\langle\psi_{k},PH^{2}P\psi_{k}\rangle

\displaystyle=\sum_{k=1}^{m}\|H\psi_{k}\|^{2}

\displaystyle\leq m\delta^{2}

and finish the proof.
∎

Appendix B
Proof of Lemma
2.3

Proof.

Without loss of generality, we may consider the case
\left|E^{\prime}\right|\leq\delta
implies
E^{\prime\prime}\geq 2
and
\theta_{s}\leq\theta_{1}\leq\theta_{2}
. Since
E^{\prime}\left(\theta_{s}\right)=0
, by assumption
E^{\prime\prime}\left(\theta_{s}\right)\geq 2
. Let
\theta_{d}
be the largest point with the following property:

E^{\prime\prime}(\theta)\geq 2\text{ for }\theta_{s}\leq\theta\leq\theta_{d}.

This implies that
E(\theta)
is an increasing function near the right of
\theta_{s}
. By definition of
\theta_{d}
, we have
E^{\prime\prime}\left(\theta_{d}+\Delta\theta_{n}\right)<2
for a sequence
\Delta\theta_{n}\to 0^{+}
; therefore
E^{\prime}\left(\theta_{d}+\Delta\theta_{n}\right)>\delta
. This inequality must hold for every
\theta>\theta_{d}
or else we would have a point
\theta^{*}>\theta_{d}
where
E^{\prime}(\theta^{*})=\delta,E^{\prime}(\theta^{*}-\Delta\theta)>\delta
for small
\Delta\theta>0
, but
E^{\prime\prime}(\theta^{*})\geq 2>0
by
|E^{\prime}(\theta^{*})|\leq\delta
and the assumption of
E
. This is impossible. Therefore

E^{\prime}(\theta)\geq\delta\text{ for }\theta>\theta_{d}.

There are three cases.

Case
1:
\theta_{s}\leq\theta_{1}<\theta_{2}\leq\theta_{d}
.

\displaystyle E(\theta_{2})-E(\theta_{1})=E^{\prime}(\theta_{1})(\theta_{2}-\theta_{1})+\frac{1}{2}E^{\prime\prime}(\xi)(\theta_{2}-\theta_{1})^{2}\geq(\theta_{2}-\theta_{1})^{2}.

Case
2:
\theta_{d}\leq\theta_{1}<\theta_{2}
.

\displaystyle E(\theta_{2})-E(\theta_{1})=E^{\prime}(\xi)(\theta_{2}-\theta_{1})\geq\delta(\theta_{2}-\theta_{1})\geq(\theta_{2}-\theta_{1})^{2}.

Case
3:
\theta_{s}\leq\theta_{1}\leq\theta_{d}\leq\theta_{2}
.

\displaystyle E(\theta_{2})-E(\theta_{1})

\displaystyle=E(\theta_{2})-E(\theta_{d})+E(\theta_{d})-E(\theta_{1})

\displaystyle\geq(\theta_{2}-\theta_{d})^{2}+(\theta_{d}-\theta_{1})^{2}

\displaystyle\geq\frac{1}{2}(\theta_{2}-\theta_{1})^{2}.

To prove the second inequality, we assume
\theta_{s}\leq\theta
(The case
\theta\leq\theta_{s}
is analogous). We need to consider two cases.

Case
1:
\theta_{s}\leq\theta\leq\theta_{d}
.

E^{\prime}(\theta)=E^{\prime}(\theta_{s})+E^{\prime\prime}(\xi)(\theta-\theta_{s})\geq\theta-\theta_{s}.

Case
2:
\theta_{d}\leq\theta
. In this case we have
E^{\prime}(\theta)\geq\delta
.

Hence we finish the proof.
∎

Appendix C
Proof of Lemma
2.4

Proof.

The local
C^{2}
smoothness of
E(\theta)
is inherited from the operators since
E(\theta^{*})
is a simple eigenvalue. Noting that
\langle\psi,\psi\rangle\equiv 1
, we have
\langle\psi,\psi^{\prime}\rangle=0
.
Differentiating the equation
E=\langle\psi,H\psi\rangle
yields

E^{\prime}=\langle\psi,H^{\prime}\psi\rangle+2\langle\psi^{\prime},H\psi\rangle=\langle\psi,H^{\prime}\psi\rangle,

(C.1)

where we use
H\psi=E\psi
and
\langle\psi,\psi^{\prime}\rangle=0
.

Now we prove
(2)
.
Differentiating (
C.1
) gives

E^{\prime\prime}=\langle\psi,H^{\prime\prime}\psi\rangle+2\langle\psi^{\prime},H^{\prime}\psi\rangle
. Thus it suffices to show

\langle\psi^{\prime},H^{\prime}\psi\rangle=-\langle H^{\prime}\psi,G^{\perp}(E)H^{\prime}\psi\rangle.

Since
\psi^{\prime}
is orthogonal to
\psi
, we have

\displaystyle\langle\psi^{\prime},H^{\prime}\psi\rangle=\langle P^{\perp}\psi^{\prime},H^{\prime}\psi\rangle=\langle G^{\perp}(E)(H-E)\psi^{\prime},H^{\prime}\psi\rangle=-\langle H^{\prime}\psi,G^{\perp}(E)H^{\prime}\psi\rangle,

where we use
G^{\perp}(E)\psi=0
and
(H^{\prime}-E^{\prime})\psi=-(H-E)\psi^{\prime}
since
(H-E)\psi\equiv 0
.

Finally,
(3)
follows from

G^{\perp}(E)=((H-E)^{\perp})^{-1}=\sum_{E^{\prime}\neq E}\frac{1}{E^{\prime}-E}P_{E^{\prime}}

and

G^{\perp\perp}(E)=((H-E)^{\perp\perp})^{-1}=\sum_{E^{\prime}\neq E,\mathcal{E}}\frac{1}{E^{\prime}-E}P_{E^{\prime}}

immediately.
∎

Appendix D
Lemmas for constructing the desired regular deformation

The desired deformation can be made by the following two lemmas:

Lemma D.1
.

Let
\theta^{*}\in\mathbb{T},E^{*}\in\mathbb{R}
and
E_{n}\in\mathcal{C}_{n}
. Then any three different points
x_{1},x_{2},x_{3}\in S_{n}(\theta^{*},E^{*})
relative to
E_{n}
satisfy

\max(\|x_{1}-x_{2}\|_{1},\|x_{2}-x_{3}\|_{1},\|x_{1}-x_{3}\|_{1})\gtrsim\delta_{n}^{-\frac{1}{2\tau}}\gg l_{n}.

The above separation also holds uniformly for
\theta
and
E
belonging to a small neighborhood (of order
\delta_{n}
) of
(\theta^{*},E^{*})
.

Proof.

By the two-monotonicity interval structure and Morse condition of
E_{n}
, it follows that
the preimage
E_{n}^{-1}(B_{\delta_{n}}(E^{*}))
has at most two connected components with length
\lesssim\delta_{n}^{\frac{1}{2}}
. The lemma follows from the Diophantine condition and pigeonhole principle immediately.
∎

Lemma D.2
.

Let
L>1
. Assume a set
S\subset\mathbb{Z}^{d}
satisfies the following condition:

Any three different points
x_{1},x_{2},x_{3}\in S
satisfy

\max(\|x_{1}-x_{2}\|_{1},\|x_{2}-x_{3}\|_{1},\|x_{1}-x_{3}\|_{1})>100L.

Then for any set
B\subset\mathbb{Z}^{d}
, there exists a deformation
B^{*}
satisfying

B\subset B^{*}\subset(B+\Lambda_{50L})=\{x\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}:\ \operatorname{dist}(x,B)\leq 50L\},

such that
(\Lambda_{L}+x)\subset B^{*}
for any
x\in S
with
(\Lambda_{L}+x)\cap B^{*}\neq\emptyset
.

Proof.

We inductively define
B_{0}:=B
,

B_{0}\subsetneqq B_{1}\subsetneqq\cdots\subsetneqq B_{N}

(D.1)

satisfying

B_{n+1}=B_{n}\bigcup\left(\bigcup_{\{x\in S:\ (\Lambda_{L}+x)\cap B_{n}\neq\emptyset\}}(\Lambda_{L}+x)\right)

for
n\geq 0
,
where
N
is the largest integer such that the “
\subsetneqq
” relationship holds in (
D.1
). We claim that
N\leq 2
. Otherwise, there exist three different points
x_{1},x_{2},x_{3}\in S
such that

(\Lambda_{L}+x_{1})\cap B_{0}\neq\emptyset,\ (\Lambda_{L}+x_{2})\cap(\Lambda_{L}+x_{1})\neq\emptyset,\ (\Lambda_{L}+x_{3})\cap(\Lambda_{L}+x_{2})\neq\emptyset.

Thus
\max(\|x_{1}-x_{2}\|_{1},\|x_{2}-x_{3}\|_{1},\|x_{1}-x_{3}\|_{1})\leq 4L,
a contradiction. Then
B^{*}:=B_{N}
is the desired deformation.
∎
